Quadratic relations between periods of connections Thanks: This research was partly supported by the PICS project TWN 8094 from CNRS. The research of J.F. was also partly supported by the grant ANR-18-CE40-0017 of Agence Nationale de la Recherche.
Javier Fresán, Claude Sabbah, Jeng-Daw Yu

Abstract
. We prove the existence of quadratic relations between periods of meromorphic flat bundles on complex manifolds with poles along a divisor with normal crossings under the assumption of “goodness”. In dimension one, for which goodness is always satisfied, we provide methods to compute the various pairings involved. In an appendix, we recall some classical results needed for the proofs.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
32G20, 34M35

1.
Introduction

Let
U
be an affine open set of the complex projective line.
For an algebraic vector bundle
V
on
U
endowed with an algebraic connection
\nabla
and a non-degenerate pairing compatible with
\nabla
, there exists a natural pairing
\mathsf{P}
, called the
period pairing
, between the first de Rham cohomology space
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(U,V)
and the first homology space
\mathrm{H}_{1}(U^{\mathrm{an}},V^{\nabla})
—also known as the twisted homology space—on the associated analytic space
U^{\mathrm{an}}

with coefficients in the local system
V^{\nabla}
of horizontal sections of
(V,\nabla)
. This pairing is obtained by integrating
1
-forms with values in
V
against
1
-cycles twisted by
V^{\nabla}
, by means of the given pairing
V\otimes V\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{O}(U)
. There also exists a similar pairing
\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{c}}
between the de Rham cohomology with compact support
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,V)
and the Borel-Moore twisted homology
\mathrm{H}_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BM}}(U^{\mathrm{an}},V^{\nabla})
. The latter pairing is less used than the former because the expression of de Rham classes with compact support is not as simple as that of de Rham classes with no support condition. Moreover,
since Borel-Moore cycles may have boundary in
\mathbb{P}^{1}\smallsetminus U
, this pairing needs a regularization procedure to be expressed.

In the case when
\nabla
has regular singularities at infinity, both pairings
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{c}}
are known to be non-degenerate, as well as the de Rham duality pairing

\mathsf{S}:\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,V)\otimes\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(U,V)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}

and the intersection pairing

\mathsf{B}:\mathrm{H}_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BM}}(U^{\mathrm{an}},V^{\nabla})\otimes\mathrm{H}_{1}(U^{\mathrm{an}},V^{\nabla})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}.

Poincaré duality, regarded as an isomorphism between cohomology and homology, implies a relation between the matrices of these pairings:

(2\pi\mathsf{i})\mathsf{B}=\mathsf{P}\cdot\mathsf{S}^{-1}\cdot{}^{t}\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{c}}.

In many interesting examples, both cohomologies
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(U,V)
and
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,V)

resp.
homologies
\mathrm{H}_{1}(U^{\mathrm{an}},V^{\nabla})
and
\mathrm{H}_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BM}}(U^{\mathrm{an}},V^{\nabla})
coincide,
and there is no distinction between
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{c}}
. This relation is then regarded as a family of polynomial relations of degree two between the entries of the period matrix
\mathsf{P}
. If
V
and
V^{\nabla}

are defined respectively over subfields
{\boldsymbol{K}}
and
{\boldsymbol{k}}
of
\mathbf{C}
, the pairings
\mathsf{B}
and
\mathsf{S}
take values in these fields and such relations take the form of an equality between a quadratic expression of the periods with coefficients in
{\boldsymbol{K}}
and an element in
(2\pi\mathsf{i}){\boldsymbol{k}}
. In a series of papers, Matsumoto et al.
[
12
,
31
,
32
,
27
]
have developed this technique and have used it to produce quadratic relations between classical special functions, see also
[
22
,
23
]
. The aforementioned articles show many interesting examples where these quadratic relations can be effectively computed. This procedure can be extended in higher dimension, giving rise to examples related to hyperplane arrangements. We refer to the book
[
2
]
where many examples are treated with details.

If one relaxes the condition of regular singularities for the connection
\nabla
, the period pairings
\mathsf{P},\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{c}}
may be degenerate because they pair spaces of possible different dimensions. In
[
38
]
, the authors have extended the quadratic relations to connections, with irregular singularities at some points of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\smallsetminus U
, obtained by adding a rational
1
-form to the trivial connection
{\mathrm{d}}
on the trivial bundle
V=\mathscr{O}_{U}
. In order to retain a perfect period pairing,
the twisted cycles have to be replaced with twisted cycles with rapid decay, which happen to coincide with those introduced by Bloch-Esnault
[
5
]
. These are supported by chains with boundary in
\mathbb{P}^{1}\smallsetminus U
that reach these boundary points in suitable directions so that the corresponding period integrals converge. It is then convenient to work with the topological space obtained by completing
U^{\mathrm{an}}
with the space of directions at each point of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\smallsetminus U
, that is, the real oriented blow-up space of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
at each point of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\smallsetminus U
.
Correspondingly, the de Rham cohomologies with and without support conditions are computed by means of the
C^{\infty}
de Rham cohomology with rapid decay and moderate growth respectively on this blow-up space, in a way very similar to that developed in
[
38
]
(see also
[
41
]
).

The main purpose of this article is to establish the existence of quadratic relations that take into account rapid decay and moderate growth properties on the real oriented blow-up space, and to give some methods to compute them for any differential equation on a Riemann surface, without any assumption on the kind of singularities it may have. We then obtain quadratic relations with enough generality in order to analyze, say, quadratic relations between Bessel moments, which are periods of symmetric powers of the Bessel differential equation
[
21
]
. Treating this example was the main motivation for developing the theory in this direction, and the reader will find in loc. cit. a much detailed example of the various notions explained in the present article.

As for the case with regular singularities, these quadratic relations also exist in higher dimensions and may be interesting when considering period pairings for irregular GKZ systems or hyperplane arrangements twisted by exponentials of rational functions for example. The general rule is to replace forms with “no support condition” (
resp.
“Borel-Moore” cycles) by forms (
resp.
cycles) with “moderate growth”, and similarly “compact support” by “rapid decay”.

Contents and organization of the article

In Section
2
, we consider the case of a differentiable manifold with corners and a differentiable vector bundle on it endowed with an integrable connection having poles along the boundary. No analyticity property is needed, but an assumption on the behaviour near the boundary of the de Rham complexes of differential forms seems to be essential (Assumptions
2.1
). Corollaries
2.14
and
2.24
provide quadratic relations in a very general form. We emphasize
middle quadratic relations
since they tend to be the most non-trivial part of quadratic relations. We revisit the period pairings introduced by Bloch and Esnault
[
5
]
in the form considered by Hien
[
24
,
25
,
26
]
. Applications to period pairings similar to those of loc. cit. in any dimension are given in
Section

2.f
. The main idea, taken from
[
24
,
25
,
26
]
, is to define local period
pairings
in the sheaf-theoretical sense, prove that they are perfect, and get for free the perfectness of the global period pairings by applying the Poincaré-Verdier duality theorem. In that case, the manifold with corners is nothing but the real oriented blow-up of a complex manifold along a divisor with normal crossings. Provided Assumptions
2.1
are satisfied, these ideas apply to the general framework for proving quadratic relations between periods.

Section
3
focuses on the case of meromorphic connections on Riemann surfaces. A formula “à la Čech” for computing the de Rham pairing is provided by Theorem
3.13
. In the case of rank-one bundles with meromorphic connection, this result already appears in Deligne’s notes
[
16
]
. We also give a formula for computing period matrices (Proposition
3.18
) that goes back, for connections with regular singularities, at least to
[
19
, Rem. 2.16]
. Quadratic relations in the sense of Matsumoto et al. are obtained in (
3.21
). The reader will find these notions illustrated in the example treated in
[
21
]
.

The appendix recalls with detailed proofs
1
1

1

The proofs are omitted in the published version.
classical results which are taken from the Séminaire Cartan
[
7
,
8
]
, the books of de Rham
[
13
,
14
]
, of Malgrange
[
39
]
, and of Kashiwara and Schapira
[
28
]
. Let us emphasize that a similar approach has already been used by Kita and Yoshida
[
33
]
in order to define and compute the Betti intersection pairing for ordinary twisted homology (see also
[
2
, Chap. 2]
). We improve their results by extending them to connections of any rank and possibly with irregular singularities.

Needless to say, the results of this article are not essentially new, but we have tried to give them with enough generality, rigor, and details so that they can be used in various situations without reproving the intermediate steps.

2.
Pairings for bundles with flat connection and quadratic relations

2.a.
Setting and notation

We consider a
C^{\infty}
manifold
M
of real dimension
m
with corners, that we assume to be connected and orientable. The boundary of
M
is denoted by
{\partial M}
, and the inclusion of the interior
M^{\circ}=M\smallsetminus{\partial M}
by
j\colon M^{\circ}\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}M
. In the neighbourhood of each point of
{\partial M}
, the pair
(M,{\partial M})
is diffeomorphic to
(\mathbf{R}_{+}{}^{p},\partial(\mathbf{R}_{+}{}^{p}))\times\mathbf{R}^{m-p}
for some integer
p\in[1,m]
. The sheaf
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{M}
of
C^{\infty}
functions (
resp.

\mathfrak{Db}_{M}
of distributions) on
M
is locally the sheaf-theoretic restriction to
\mathbf{R}_{+}{}^{p}\times\mathbf{R}^{m-p}
of the sheaf of
C^{\infty}
functions (
resp.
distributions) on
\mathbf{R}^{m}
. We will also consider the sheaf
\mathscr{C}_{M}^{\infty}(*)
of
C^{\infty}
functions on
M^{\circ}
having at most poles along
{\partial M}
. The de Rham complex
(\mathscr{E}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}_{M},{\mathrm{d}})
of
C^{\infty}
differential forms on
M
is a resolution of the constant sheaf
\mathbf{C}_{M}
. The complex of currents
(\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)
, denoted homologically, can be regarded cohomologically as
(\mathfrak{Db}_{M}^{m-{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},{\mathrm{d}})
. Recall (see Appendix
B
) that a current of homological index
q
is a linear form on test differential forms of degree
q
. Then the inclusion of complexes
(\mathscr{E}^{m-{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}_{M},{\mathrm{d}})\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}(\mathfrak{Db}_{M}^{m-{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},{\mathrm{d}})
is a quasi-isomorphism. In other words,
(\mathfrak{Db}_{M}^{m-{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},{\mathrm{d}})
is a resolution of
\mathbf{C}_{M}[m]
. We refer the reader to Appendix
B
for the notation and results concerning currents with moderate growth. The
results
in this section are a mere adaptation of those contained in de Rham’s book
[
13
,
14
]
(see Chapter IV in
[
14
]
). In order to simplify the discussion, and since we are only interested in this case, we assume throughout this section that
M
is moreover
compact
.

2.b.
De Rham formalism for quadratic relations

Setting and the main assumption

We keep the assumptions and notations as in Section
2.a
. Let
(\mathscr{V},\nabla)
be a locally free
\mathscr{C}_{M}^{\infty}(*)
-module of finite rank on
M
endowed with a flat connection
\nabla
and a
flat non-degenerate
pairing

\left\langle{{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}\right\rangle:\mathscr{V}\otimes\mathscr{V}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{C}_{M}^{\infty}(*),

i.e., compatible with the connections. It induces a self-duality

\lambda:\mathscr{V}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathscr{V}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}

which factorizes
\left\langle{{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}\right\rangle
as the composition

\mathscr{V}\otimes\mathscr{V}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\kern 4.0pt1\otimes\lambda\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1\otimes\lambda}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}\mathscr{V}\otimes\mathscr{V}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\kern 4.0pt({\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle({\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}\mathscr{C}_{M}^{\infty}(*),

where
({\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})
denotes the natural duality pairing.
We can define the de Rham complexes of
(\mathscr{V},\nabla)
with respectively
\mathscr{C}_{M}^{\infty,\mathrm{rd}}
and
\mathscr{C}_{M}^{\infty,\mathrm{mod}}
coefficients (see Definition
B.1
). Since
\nabla
has only poles as possible singularities along
{\partial M}
, the complexes

\DR^{\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V},\nabla)=(\mathscr{E}_{M}^{\mathrm{rd},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}\otimes\mathscr{V},\nabla)\quad\text{and}\quad\DR^{\mathrm{mod}}(\mathscr{V},\nabla)=(\mathscr{E}_{M}^{\mathrm{mod},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}\otimes\mathscr{V},\nabla)

are well-defined.
The de Rham cohomologies with rapid decay
\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V})
and the de Rham cohomologies with moderate growth
\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{mod}}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V})
are defined to be the hypercohomologies of these respective complexes, which are equal to the cohomologies of the global sections. We make the following assumptions.

Assumption 2.1
.

(1)

The de Rham complexes
\DR^{\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V},\nabla)
and
\DR^{\mathrm{mod}}(\mathscr{V},\nabla)
have cohomology sheaves concentrated in degree
0
, denoted respectively by
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}
and
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}
.

(2)

The natural pairings
defined by means of
\left\langle{{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}\right\rangle:\mathscr{V}\otimes\mathscr{V}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{M}(*)

and of the wedge product:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}:\DR^{\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V},\nabla)\otimes\DR^{\mathrm{mod}}(\mathscr{V},\nabla)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(\mathscr{E}^{\mathrm{rd},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}_{M},{\mathrm{d}}),

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}_{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}:\DR^{\mathrm{mod}}(\mathscr{V},\nabla)\otimes\DR^{\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V},\nabla)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(\mathscr{E}^{\mathrm{rd},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}_{M},{\mathrm{d}})

are perfect (see Example
C.9
), i.e., the pairings induced on the
\mathscr{H}^{0}
are perfect:

\displaystyle\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}\otimes\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}j_{!}\mathbf{C}_{M^{\circ}},

\displaystyle\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}\otimes\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}j_{!}\mathbf{C}_{M^{\circ}}.

One can drop the assumption on the existence of a non-degenerate pairing
\left\langle{{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}\right\rangle
and use
({\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})
instead. One can easily adapt the results of this section by distinguishing between
\mathscr{V}
and
\mathscr{V}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}
.

The de Rham pairings

Verdier duality implies (Proposition
C.4
) that the natural pairings

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{m-r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}})\otimes\mathrm{H}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}})

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}^{m}_{\mathrm{c}}(M^{\circ},\mathbf{C})\simeq\mathbf{C},

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{m-r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}})\otimes\mathrm{H}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}})

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}^{m}_{\mathrm{c}}(M^{\circ},\mathbf{C})\simeq\mathbf{C}

are non-degenerate. We will interpret these pairings in terms of the de Rham cohomologies
\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}^{m-r}(M,\mathscr{V})
and
\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{mod}}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V})
to which these cohomologies can respectively be identified by means of Assumption
2.1
(
1
). We note that the diagram

(2.3)

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 64.14731pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-64.14731pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 69.01794pt\raise 6.4472pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.4139pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{Q}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 106.82643pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\kern-64.14731pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\DR^{\mathrm{mod}}(\mathscr{V},\nabla)\otimes\DR^{\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V},\nabla)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.44434pt\raise-8.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 69.01794pt\raise-26.54282pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.4139pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{Q}_{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 106.82643pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 106.82643pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\mathscr{E}^{\mathrm{rd},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}_{M},{\mathrm{d}})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

commutes in the following sense. For any rapid-decay forms
\omega_{1}^{\mathrm{rd}}
and
\omega_{2}^{\mathrm{rd}}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{V}
, we let
\omega_{1}^{\mathrm{mod}},\omega_{2}^{\mathrm{mod}}
be the same forms considered as forms with moderate growth. Commutativity means that for any such
\omega_{1}^{\mathrm{rd}},\omega_{2}^{\mathrm{rd}}
the pairings satisfy

\mathsf{Q}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}(\omega_{1}^{\mathrm{rd}},\omega_{2}^{\mathrm{mod}})=\mathsf{Q}_{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}(\omega_{1}^{\mathrm{mod}},\omega_{2}^{\mathrm{rd}}).

For any
r\geqslant 0
, let
\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}
be the global de Rham pairing

\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}:\Gamma(M,\mathscr{E}_{M}^{\mathrm{rd},m-r}\otimes\mathscr{V})^{\nabla}\otimes\Gamma(M,\mathscr{E}_{M}^{\mathrm{mod},r}\otimes\mathscr{V})^{\nabla}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}

obtained by restricting to horizontal sections the composition of
\Gamma(M,\mathsf{Q}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}})
with integration
\int_{M}:\mathscr{E}_{M}^{\mathrm{rd},m}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}
. Define
\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}
similarly. Since rapid decay forms vanish on
{\partial M}
, it follows from Stokes formula that
\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}
(
resp.

\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}
) vanishes when one of the terms is a coboundary. We thus obtain from the perfectness of (
2.2
):

Corollary 2.4
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
, the induced pairing

\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}:\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}^{m-r}(M,\mathscr{V})\otimes\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{mod}}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}

is perfect, and so is
\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}\,
.∎

Twisted currents with rapid decay and moderate growth

We consider the following complexes of currents with coefficients in
\mathscr{V}
(see (
B.3
) for the boundary operator):

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

twisted currents with rapid decay

(\mathfrak{C}^{\mathrm{rd}}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{V}),\partial)=(\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\mathrm{mod}},\partial)\otimes\DR^{\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V},\nabla)\simeq(\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\mathrm{mod}},\partial)\otimes\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}},

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

twisted currents with moderate growth

(\mathfrak{C}^{\mathrm{mod}}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{V}),\partial)=(\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\mathrm{mod}},\partial)\otimes\DR^{\mathrm{mod}}(\mathscr{V},\nabla)\simeq(\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\mathrm{mod}},\partial)\otimes\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}.

We define respectively
the de Rham homology with rapid decay and with moderate growth with coefficients in
\mathscr{V}
as

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}^{\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}_{r}\bigl(\Gamma(M,\mathfrak{C}^{\mathrm{rd}}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{V}))\bigr),

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}^{\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}_{r}\bigl(\Gamma(M,\mathfrak{C}^{\mathrm{mod}}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{V}))\bigr)

(recall that
M
is compact). For example, when
(\mathscr{V},\nabla)=(\mathscr{C}_{M}^{\infty}(*),{\mathrm{d}})
, rapid decay and moderate de Rham homologies are respectively the homology and the Borel-Moore homology of
M^{\circ}
(see Remark
B.13
).

The Poincaré isomorphisms

For the sake of simplicity, we will write the complex of currents cohomologically via the relation
\mathfrak{C}_{M,q}^{\mathrm{mod}}=\mathfrak{C}_{M}^{\mathrm{mod},m-q}
, so that we have a quasi-isomorphism
\mathbf{C}_{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\mathfrak{C}_{M}^{\mathrm{mod},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},\partial)
(see Proposition
B.10
). This leads to Poincaré isomorphisms in the bounded derived category
\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbf{C}_{M})
, making the following diagram commutative:

(2.5)

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 32.98512pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-29.16219pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern By taking hypercohomologies (recall that
M
is compact) and switching back to homology on the right-hand side, we thus obtain global Poincaré isomorphisms

(2.6)

\begin{split}\eucal{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V}):\mathrm{H}^{m-r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}^{\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V}),\\
\eucal{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{mod}}(\mathscr{V}):\mathrm{H}^{m-r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}^{\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V}).\end{split}

De Rham period pairings

We define the de Rham period pairing

(2.7)

\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{\mathrm{dR}}:(\mathfrak{C}^{\mathrm{rd},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}_{M}(\mathscr{V}),\partial)\otimes\DR^{\mathrm{mod}}(\mathscr{V},\nabla)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(\mathfrak{C}_{M}^{\mathrm{rd},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},\partial)

as follows:
for a
q
-dimensional current
T_{q}=T^{m-q}
with moderate growth,
an
r
-form
\eta^{r}
with rapid decay,
an
s
-form
\omega^{s}
with moderate growth
and local sections
v,w
of
\mathscr{V}
,
the pairing of the local sections

T_{q}\otimes\eta^{r}\otimes v
and
\omega^{s}\otimes w
is the current

\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{\mathrm{dR}}(T_{q}\otimes\eta^{r}\otimes v,\omega^{s}\otimes w)=T_{q}\otimes(\langle v,w\rangle\cdot\eta^{r}\wedge\omega^{s})

of dimension
q-r-s
.
Compatibility with differentials follows from (
B.3
). It has rapid decay since
\langle v,w\rangle
has at most a pole (and hence moderate growth), and thus
\langle v,w\rangle\cdot\eta^{r}\wedge\omega^{s}
has rapid decay. Note that, if
T_{q}
is
C^{\infty}
, then we recover
\mathsf{Q}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}
.

Similarly we define the period pairing

(2.8)

\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{\mathrm{dR}}:(\mathfrak{C}^{\mathrm{mod},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}_{M}(\mathscr{V}),\partial)\otimes\DR^{\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V},\nabla)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(\mathfrak{C}_{M}^{\mathrm{rd},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},\partial).

Then the following (cohomological) diagram commutes:

as well as a similar diagram for
\mathsf{Q}_{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}
. For each
r\geqslant 0
, let
\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}
and
\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}
be the global period pairings

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}:\Gamma(M,\mathfrak{C}_{M}^{\mathrm{rd},m-r}(\mathscr{V}))^{\partial}\otimes\Gamma(M,\mathscr{E}_{M}^{\mathrm{mod},r}\otimes\mathscr{V})^{\nabla}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Gamma(M,\mathfrak{C}_{M}^{\mathrm{rd},m})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}:\Gamma(M,\mathfrak{C}_{M}^{\mathrm{mod},m-r}(\mathscr{V}))^{\partial}\otimes\Gamma(M,\mathscr{E}_{M}^{\mathrm{rd},r}\otimes\mathscr{V})^{\nabla}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Gamma(M,\mathfrak{C}_{M}^{\mathrm{rd},m})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}

obtained by evaluating on the constant function
1
the zero-dimensional rapid-decay current provided by
\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{\mathrm{dR}}
and
\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{\mathrm{dR}}
respectively. Then, according to Stokes formula,
\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}
and
\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}
vanish if one of the terms is a boundary, and hence induce pairings

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}:\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}^{\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})\otimes\mathrm{H}^{r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V})

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}:\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}^{\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V})\otimes\mathrm{H}^{r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}.

It is then clear that the following diagram commutes (and similarly for
\mathsf{Q}^{r}_{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}
):

We then deduce from Corollary
2.4
:

Corollary 2.11
.

The period pairings
\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}
and
\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},m-r}
are perfect.∎

De Rham intersection pairing

We can apply once more the Poincaré isomorphisms to (
2.9
), and get the morphism
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}
(
resp.

\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}
) making the following (cohomological) diagram commute:

\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}_{M}^{\mathrm{mod},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}(\mathscr{V})\otimes\DR^{\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V},\nabla)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\eucal{P}^{\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V})}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{(\mathfrak{C}_{M}^{\mathrm{rd},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},\partial)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}}
\textstyle{(\mathfrak{C}_{M}^{\mathrm{rd},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},\partial)}
