0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},m-r}^{\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V})\otimes\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}^{\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 71.95412pt\raise-25.72835pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.22836pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{B}_{{\mathrm{dR}},m-r}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 118.62317pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 118.62317pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\phantom{.}\mathbf{C}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

For cohomology classes
[\eta^{r}]\in\mathrm{H}^{m-r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})
and
[\omega^{m-r}]\in\mathrm{H}^{r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V})
, we have by definition

\mathsf{Q}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}^{r}([\eta^{r}],[\omega^{m-r}])=\mathsf{B}_{{\mathrm{dR}},m-r}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}(\eucal{P}^{\mathrm{rd}}_{m-r}([\eta^{r}]),\eucal{P}^{\mathrm{mod}}_{r}([\omega^{m-r}])).

Corollary 2.13
.

The de Rham intersection pairings
\mathsf{B}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}},\mathsf{B}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}
are perfect for each
r\geqslant 0
.

Proof.

This follows from the perfectness of the period pairings (see Corollary
2.11
).
∎

Quadratic relations

We assume that the cohomology and homology vector spaces we consider are finite-dimensional and we fix bases of these spaces. We denote by the same letter a pairing and its matrix with respect to the corresponding bases, and we use the matrix notation in which, for a pairing
\mathsf{A}
, we also denote
\mathsf{A}(x\otimes y)
by
{}^{t}x\cdot\mathsf{A}\cdot y
,
and the transpose of the matrix
\mathsf{A}
by
{}^{t}\mathsf{A}
.

Corollary 2.14

(Quadratic relations)
.

The matrices of the pairings satisfy the following relations:

{}^{t}\mathsf{B}_{{\mathrm{dR}},m-r}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}=\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}\cdot(\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}})^{-1}\cdot{}^{t}\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},m-r}.

Proof.

In terms of matrices, the commutations (
2.10
) and (
2.12
) read

{}^{t}\eucal{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V})\cdot\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}=\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}},\quad\mathsf{B}_{{\mathrm{dR}},m-r}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}\cdot\eucal{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V})=\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},m-r},

and, by eliminating
\eucal{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V})
, we obtain the sought for relation.
∎

See Remark
2.17
for a justification of the terminology “quadratic relations”.

Remark 2.15

(Quadratic relations in presence of
\pm
-symmetry)
.

Let us assume that
\left\langle{{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}\right\rangle
is either symmetric or skew-symmetric, a property that we call
\pm
-symmetric.
Let us denote by

{}^{t}\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}:\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{mod}}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V})\otimes\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}^{m-r}(M,\mathscr{V})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}

the pairing defined (with obvious notation) by

{}^{t}\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}(h^{r},h^{m-r})=\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}(h^{m-r},h^{r}),

and let
{}^{t}\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}
be defined similarly. If
\eta^{m-r}
is a rapid decay
(m-r)
-form and
\omega^{r}
an
r
-form with moderate growth, and if
v,w
are local sections of
\mathscr{V}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}^{m-r}(\eta^{m-r}\otimes v,\omega^{r}\otimes w)

\displaystyle=\langle v,w\rangle\cdot\eta^{m-r}\wedge\omega^{r}

\displaystyle=\pm(-1)^{r(m-r)}\langle w,v\rangle\omega^{r}\wedge\eta^{m-r}

\displaystyle=\pm(-1)^{r(m-r)}\mathsf{Q}_{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}^{r}(\omega^{r}\otimes w,\eta^{m-r}\otimes v).

Passing to cohomology, we find

{}^{t}\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}=\pm(-1)^{r(m-r)}\mathsf{Q}_{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}^{r},\qquad{}^{t}\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}=\pm(-1)^{r(m-r)}\mathsf{Q}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}^{r}.

For the de Rham intersection pairing, we similarly find

{}^{t}\mathsf{B}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},m-r}=\pm(-1)^{r(m-r)}\mathsf{B}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}.

As a consequence, the quadratic relations read, in term of matrices,

\pm(-1)^{r(m-r)}\mathsf{B}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}=\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}\cdot(\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}})^{-1}\cdot{}^{t}\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},m-r}.

2.c.
Middle quadratic relations

For each degree
r
, we set

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{mid}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}(M,\mathscr{V})

\displaystyle=\image\bigl[\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{rd}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}(M,\mathscr{V})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{mod}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}(M,\mathscr{V})\bigr],

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{m-r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(M,\mathscr{V})

\displaystyle=\image\bigl[\mathrm{H}^{m-r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}^{m-r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V})\bigr],

where the maps are induced by the natural inclusion
\mathscr{C}_{M}^{\infty,\mathrm{rd}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{C}_{M}^{\infty,\mathrm{mod}}
.
From (
2.3
) one deduces that
\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}
and
\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}
induce the same
non-degenerate
pairing

\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{{\mathrm{mid}}}:\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}^{m-r}(M,\mathscr{V})\otimes\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}.

Similarly, according to (
2.5
),
\eucal{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V})
and
\eucal{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{mod}}(\mathscr{V})
induce the same isomorphism

\eucal{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{mid}}(\mathscr{V}):\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}^{m-r}(M,\mathscr{V})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}^{\mathrm{mid}}(M,\mathscr{V}).

It follows that
\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}
and
\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}
induce the same
non-degenerate
period pairing

\mathsf{P}^{{\mathrm{mid}}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}:\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}^{\mathrm{mid}}(M,\mathscr{V})\otimes\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}^{m-r}(M,\mathscr{V})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}.

In the same way,
\mathsf{B}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}
induce the same
non-degenerate
intersection pairing

\mathsf{B}^{{\mathrm{mid}}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}:\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}^{\mathrm{mid}}(M,\mathscr{V})\otimes\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{m-r}}^{\mathrm{mid}}(M,\mathscr{V})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}.

We conclude:

Corollary 2.16

(Middle quadratic relations)
.

These pairings are non-degenerate and their matrices satisfy the following relations:

{}^{t}\mathsf{B}_{{\mathrm{dR}},m-r}^{{\mathrm{mid}}}=\mathsf{P}^{{\mathrm{mid}}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}\cdot(\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{{\mathrm{mid}}})^{-1}\cdot{}^{t}\mathsf{P}^{{\mathrm{mid}}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},m-r}.

Remark 2.17

(
\pm
-Symmetry and quadratic relations)
.

Under the symmetry assumption of Remark
2.15
, the following relations hold:

{}^{t}\mathsf{B}_{{\mathrm{dR}},m-r}^{{\mathrm{mid}}}=\pm(-1)^{r(m-r)}\mathsf{B}^{{\mathrm{mid}}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r},\quad{}^{t}\mathsf{Q}^{r}_{{\mathrm{mid}}}=\pm(-1)^{r(m-r)}\mathsf{Q}_{{\mathrm{mid}}}^{m-r},

and the relations of Corollary
2.16
read

\pm(-1)^{r(m-r)}\mathsf{B}^{{\mathrm{mid}}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}=\mathsf{P}^{{\mathrm{mid}}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}\cdot(\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{{\mathrm{mid}}})^{-1}\cdot{}^{t}\mathsf{P}^{{\mathrm{mid}}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},m-r}.

If
r=m-r
, we can regard these formulas as
algebraic relations of degree two
on the entries of the matrix
\mathsf{P}^{{\mathrm{mid}}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}
, with coefficients in the entries of
\mathsf{B}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}^{{\mathrm{mid}}}
and
\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{{\mathrm{mid}}}
. This justifies the name “quadratic relations” which, strictly speaking, only applies to the middle periods in middle dimension.

Caveat 2.18

(On the notation
{\mathrm{mid}}
)
.

The terminology “middle quadratic relation” and the associated notation
{\mathrm{mid}}
could be confusing,
as it is usually associated with the notion of intermediate (or middle) extension of a sheaf across a divisor.
Here, we use it in the naive sense above, and we do not claim in general (that is, in the setting of Section
2.f
below) any precise relation with the usual notion of intermediate extension. However, we will check that both notions coincide in complex dimension one (see (
3.1
)).

2.d.
Betti formalism for quadratic relations

In the topological setting we replace the complex of currents
(\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\mathrm{mod}},\partial)
with the complex of sheaves of relative singular chains
(\eucal{C}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)
. We will consider the latter sheaves with coefficients in
\mathbf{C}
since we will compare them with sheaves of currents. We refer to Appendix
A
for basic properties of this complex, and we recall (see e.g.
[
25
, p. 14]
) that
(\eucal{C}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)
, when regarded cohomologically by setting
\eucal{C}_{M,{\partial M}}^{m-{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}=\eucal{C}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
, is a homotopically fine resolution of
\mathbf{C}_{M}
. Proposition
A.17
enables us to replace the complex of sheaves of singular chains with that of piecewise smooth singular chains, that we will denote in the same way.

Betti period pairings

Integration along a piecewise smooth singular chain in
M
of a test form with rapid decay along
{\partial M}

defines
a morphism of chain complexes
(\eucal{C}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\mathrm{mod}},\partial)
: indeed, if
c
is a
q
-dimensional chain and
\omega
a form of degree
q
on
M^{\circ}
with rapid decay,
\int_{c}\omega=0
if
c
is supported in
{\partial M}
; compatibility with
\partial
follows from the Stokes formula. This morphism is a quasi-isomorphism since it induces an isomorphism on the unique non-zero homology sheaf of both complexes; it yields a morphism between two resolutions of
\mathbf{C}_{M}
taken in homological degree
m
.

We introduce (see Section
A
) the following complexes of singular chains:

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

(\eucal{C}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}),\partial)=(\eucal{C}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)\otimes\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}
: chains with coefficients in
\mathscr{V}
and rapid decay,

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

(\eucal{C}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}),\partial)=(\eucal{C}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)\otimes\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}
: chains with coefficients in
\mathscr{V}
and moderate growth.

We also set

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{r}^{\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}_{r}\bigl(\Gamma(\eucal{C}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}),\partial)\bigr)

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{r}^{\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}_{r}\bigl(\Gamma(\eucal{C}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}),\partial)\bigr).

We thus have quasi-isomorphisms of chain complexes

(2.19)

\begin{split}(\eucal{C}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}),\partial)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V}),\partial),\\
(\eucal{C}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}),\partial)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\mathrm{mod}}(\mathscr{V}),\partial),\end{split}

which induce isomorphisms

(2.20)

\mathrm{H}_{r}^{\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}^{\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{H}_{r}^{\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}^{\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V}).

Composing (
2.7
) and (
2.8
) with these morphisms gives back the period pairings as those defined in
[
25
]
, that we denote by
\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{r}
and
\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{r}
. In particular, these Betti period pairings are perfect. Notice that, by means of these identifications, the Poincaré-de Rham isomorphisms (
2.6
) correspond to the Poincaré isomorphisms, denoted similarly:

(2.21)

\begin{split}\eucal{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V}):\mathrm{H}^{m-r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{H}_{r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}),\\
\eucal{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{mod}}(\mathscr{V}):\mathrm{H}^{m-r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{H}_{r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}).\end{split}

According to Propositions
A.14
and
A.17
, the homology spaces
\mathrm{H}_{r}^{\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})
(
resp.

\mathrm{H}_{r}^{\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V})
) can be computed as the homology of the chain complexes

(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}^{\mathrm{sm}}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}})),\partial)\quad(\text{resp.}\kern 3.00003pt(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}^{\mathrm{sm}}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}})),\partial))

of piecewise smooth singular chains with coefficients in
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}
(
resp.

\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}
).

On the one hand, let
\sigma:\Delta_{r}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}M
be a piecewise smooth singular simplex (that we can assume not contained in
{\partial M}
since we only consider relative simplices), and let
v
be a section of
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}
in the neighbourhood of
|\sigma|
, so that
\sigma\otimes v
belongs to
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\mathrm{sm}}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}})
. On the other hand, note that, by using a partition of unity, any section in
\Gamma(M,\mathscr{E}_{M}^{\mathrm{mod},r}\otimes\mathscr{V})
is a sum of terms
\omega^{r}\otimes w
, where
w
is a section of
\mathscr{V}

in the neighbourhood of the support of the
C^{\infty}

r
-form
\omega^{r}
. Then
\langle v,w\rangle
is a
C^{\infty}
function on the open set where both sections are defined and has rapid decay along
{\partial M}
, so that
\langle v,w\rangle\cdot\omega^{r}
is a
C^{\infty}
-form with rapid decay, and hence integrable along
\sigma
. We have

\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{r}(\sigma\otimes v,\omega^{r}\otimes w)=\int_{\sigma}\langle v,w\rangle\cdot\omega^{r}=\int_{\Delta_{r}}\sigma^{*}\langle v,w\rangle\cdot\sigma^{*}\omega^{r}.

This interpretation makes the link between the approaches in
[
5
]
and
[
25
]
. A similar interpretation holds for
\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{r}
: now
\langle v,w\rangle
has only moderate growth, but
\omega^{r}
has rapid decay, so that
\langle v,w\rangle\cdot\omega^{r}
remains a
C^{\infty}
-form with rapid decay.

Existence of a
{\boldsymbol{k}}
-structure

Let us fix a subfield
{\boldsymbol{k}}
of
\mathbf{C}
. The sheaves
\eucal{C}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
are now considered with coefficients in
\mathbf{Q}
. We will make explicit the
{\boldsymbol{k}}
-structure on the various cohomology groups occurring in the de Rham model, provided that a
{\boldsymbol{k}}
-structure exists on the underlying sheaves. If we are given a
{\boldsymbol{k}}
-structure
(\mathscr{V}^{\nabla})_{\boldsymbol{k}}
of
\mathscr{V}^{\nabla}
, we enrich Assumptions
2.1
as follows.

Assumption 2.22
.

There exist subsheaves
\mathscr{V}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\mathrm{rd}}\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}\mathscr{V}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\mathrm{mod}}\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}j_{*}(\mathscr{V}^{\nabla})_{\boldsymbol{k}}
of
{\boldsymbol{k}}
-vector spaces of
j_{*}\mathscr{V}^{\nabla}
and a non-degenerate pairing
\left\langle{{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}\right\rangle_{\boldsymbol{k}}
on
(\mathscr{V}^{\nabla})_{\boldsymbol{k}}
giving rise to
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}j_{*}\mathscr{V}^{\nabla}
and
\left\langle{{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}\right\rangle
after tensoring with
\mathbf{C}
.

As a consequence, the perfect pairings
\mathscr{H}^{0}\mathsf{Q}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}
and
\mathscr{H}^{0}\mathsf{Q}_{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}
in Assumption
2.1
(
2
) are defined over
{\boldsymbol{k}}
:

\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\otimes\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}j_{!}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{M^{\circ}},\quad\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\otimes\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}j_{!}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{M^{\circ}}.

In particular,
\mathscr{H}^{0}
of the commutative diagram (
2.3
) is defined over
{\boldsymbol{k}}
. Furthermore, the de Rham isomorphism induces a
{\boldsymbol{k}}
-structure on the de Rham cohomology groups, by setting for example
\mathrm{H}^{r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\mathrm{H}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}_{\boldsymbol{k}})
via the de Rham isomorphism
\mathrm{H}^{r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{H}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}})
. The pairings of Corollary
2.4
are defined over
{\boldsymbol{k}}
and correspond to Poincaré-Verdier duality over
{\boldsymbol{k}}
.

The
{\boldsymbol{k}}
-structures on rapid decay and moderate growth cycles
are obtained by means of the complexes
(\eucal{C}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)\otimes\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
and
(\eucal{C}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)\otimes\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
. In other words, we set

\mathrm{H}_{r}^{\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\mathrm{H}_{r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}),\ \text{etc.}

The Poincaré isomorphisms (
2.21
) are then defined over
{\boldsymbol{k}}
.

Proposition 2.23
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
and
2.22
, let
(v_{i})_{i}
be a basis of
\mathrm{H}^{m-r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})
and let
(v_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})_{i}
be the
\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}
-dual basis of
\mathrm{H}^{r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V})
. The
{\boldsymbol{k}}
-vector space
\mathrm{H}^{m-r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})_{\boldsymbol{k}}
is the
{\boldsymbol{k}}
-subspace of
\mathrm{H}^{m-r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})
consisting of the elements

\sum_{i}\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}(\alpha,v_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})\cdot v_{i},

where
\alpha
runs in
\mathrm{H}_{r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}_{\boldsymbol{k}})
. A similar assertion holds for
\mathrm{H}^{m-r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V})_{\boldsymbol{k}}
.

Proof.

Let
e\in\mathrm{H}^{m-r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})
be an element written as

e=\sum_{i}\mathsf{Q}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}^{m-r}(e,v_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})\cdot v_{i}.

Let us set
\alpha=\eucal{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{rd}}(e)\in\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}^{\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})
. According to (
2.10
), we can rewrite
e
as

e=\sum_{i}\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}(\alpha,v_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})\cdot v_{i}.

The class
e
belongs to
\mathrm{H}^{m-r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})_{\boldsymbol{k}}
if and only if,
when regarded as in
\mathrm{H}^{m-r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}})
,
it belongs to
\mathrm{H}^{m-r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}_{\boldsymbol{k}})
, equivalently, its image
\alpha
by the Poincaré isomorphism
\eucal{P}_{r}^{\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V})
belongs to
\mathrm{H}_{r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}_{\boldsymbol{k}})
. The assertion of the proposition follows from the above identifications.
∎

Middle quadratic relations

The Betti intersection pairing

\mathsf{B}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{r}:\mathrm{H}_{r}^{\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})\otimes\mathrm{H}_{m-r}^{\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}

is defined from the de Rham intersection pairing
\mathsf{B}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}
(see Corollary
2.13
) by composing it with the isomorphisms (
2.20
). It is thus a
perfect pairing
. A similar result holds for
\mathsf{B}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{r}
.

From Corollary
2.16
we obtain immediately (and of course similarly for the rapid decay and moderate growth analogues):

Corollary 2.24
.

The middle Betti period pairings satisfy the quadratic relations of Corollary
2.16
, where the de Rham intersection pairing
\mathsf{B}^{\mathrm{mid}}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}
is replaced with the Betti intersection pairing
\mathsf{B}^{\mathrm{mid}}_{r}
. That is, in term of matrices,

{}^{t}\mathsf{B}^{\mathrm{mid}}_{m-r}=\mathsf{P}^{{\mathrm{mid}}}_{r}\cdot(\mathsf{Q}_{\mathrm{mid}}^{m-r})^{-1}\cdot{}^{t}\mathsf{P}^{{\mathrm{mid}}}_{m-r}.

Under the symmetry assumption of Remark
2.17
, it reads

\pm(-1)^{r(m-r)}\mathsf{B}^{{\mathrm{mid}}}_{r}=\mathsf{P}^{{\mathrm{mid}}}_{r}\cdot(\mathsf{Q}_{\mathrm{mid}}^{m-r})^{-1}\cdot{}^{t}\mathsf{P}^{{\mathrm{mid}}}_{m-r}.

Computation of the Betti intersection pairing

Let us assume that
(M,{\partial M})
is endowed with a simplicial decomposition
\mathscr{T}
such that the sheaves
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}},\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}
satisfy Assumption
A.18
and are locally constant on
M^{\circ}
. Then we can
replace
the complex
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\mathrm{sm}}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}})),\partial)
, respectively the complex
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\mathrm{sm}}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}})),\partial)
, with the corresponding simplicial chain complex
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\triangle}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}})),\partial)
, respectively
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{M,{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\square}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}})),\partial)
(see Section
A.f
).

The Betti intersection pairing can easily be computed in the framework of simplicial chain complexes under this assumption. Indeed, choose an orientation for each simplex (it is natural to assume that the maximal-dimensional simplices have the orientation induced by that of
M
) and an orientation for each dual cell
\overline{D}(\sigma)
. Let
\sigma_{r}\otimes v
be a simplex of dimension
r
with coefficient
v
in
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}
(
\sigma_{r}\not\subset{\partial M}
) and let
\overline{D}(\sigma^{\prime}_{r})\otimes w
be a cell of codimension
r
with coefficient
w
in
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}
for some simplex
\sigma^{\prime}_{r}
of
\mathscr{T}
(possibly
\sigma^{\prime}_{r}\subset{\partial M}
). We regard them as currents with rapid decay and moderate growth respectively, according to (
2.19
).

Proposition 2.25

(Computation of the Betti intersection pairing)
.

With these assumptions,

\mathsf{B}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{r}(\sigma_{r}\otimes v,\overline{D}(\sigma^{\prime}_{r})\otimes w)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }\sigma^{\prime}_{r}\neq\sigma_{r},\\
\varepsilon(\sigma_{r})\cdot\langle v,w\rangle(\widehat{\sigma}_{r})&\text{if }\sigma^{\prime}_{r}=\sigma_{r},\end{cases}

where
\widehat{\sigma}_{r}
is the barycenter of
\sigma_{r}
and
\varepsilon(\sigma_{r})=\pm 1
is the orientation change between
\sigma_{r}\times\overline{D}(\sigma_{r})
and
M
.

Proof.

By definition, we have

(2.26)

\mathsf{B}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{r}(\sigma_{r}\otimes v,\overline{D}(\sigma^{\prime}_{r})\otimes w)=\langle v,w\rangle\cdot\mathsf{B}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}(\sigma_{r},\overline{D}(\sigma^{\prime}_{r})),

where
\mathsf{B}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}
corresponds to de Rham’s definition of the Kronecker index (see
[
14
, §20]
).

For any
r
-dimensional simplex
\sigma^{\prime}_{r}
of
\mathscr{T}
,
\sigma^{\prime}_{r}
is the only
r
-dimensional simplex of
\mathscr{T}
physically intersected by the cell
\overline{D}(\sigma^{\prime}_{r})
(see Lemma
A.22
). Hence, given
\sigma_{r}\!\not\subset\!{\partial M}
, we have the equality
\mathsf{B}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}(\sigma_{r},\overline{D}(\sigma^{\prime}_{r}))=0
unless
\sigma^{\prime}_{r}=\sigma_{r}
and we are reduced to computing (
2.26
) in that case. This computation, done by de Rham (see
[
14
, p. 85–86]
), is recalled in the appendix (Proposition
B.7
). The current
\mathsf{B}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}(\sigma_{r},\overline{D}(\sigma_{r}))
is supported on
\widehat{\sigma}_{r}=\sigma_{r}\cap\overline{D}(\sigma_{r})
and has coefficient
\varepsilon(\sigma_{r})
, so that

\langle v,w\rangle\cdot\mathsf{B}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}(\sigma_{r},\overline{D}(\sigma_{r}))=\langle v,w\rangle(\widehat{\sigma}_{r})\cdot\mathsf{B}_{{\mathrm{dR}},r}(\sigma_{r},\overline{D}(\sigma_{r}))=\varepsilon(\sigma_{r})\langle v,w\rangle(\widehat{\sigma}_{r}).\qed

2.e.
Period realizations

We keep the setting and notation of Section
2.d
. Let
{\boldsymbol{K}},{\boldsymbol{k}}
be two subfields of
\mathbf{C}
. The abelian category
\mathsf{Per}_{{\boldsymbol{K}},{\boldsymbol{k}}}
of
({\boldsymbol{K}},{\boldsymbol{k}})
-period structures
has objects consisting of pairs of finite-dimensional
{\boldsymbol{K}}
, respectively
{\boldsymbol{k}}
, vector spaces
(V_{{\mathrm{dR}}},V_{\mathrm{B}})
together with an isomorphism
\mathrm{comp}:\mathbf{C}\otimes_{\boldsymbol{k}}V_{\mathrm{B}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{C}\otimes_{\boldsymbol{K}}V_{\mathrm{dR}}
. The morphisms are the natural ones. It is convenient to consider this category from a dual point of view. Namely, we consider the category
\mathsf{Per}_{{\boldsymbol{K}},{\boldsymbol{k}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}
whose objects are triples
(V_{\mathrm{dR}},V_{\mathrm{B}},\mathsf{P})
, where
\mathsf{P}:(\mathbf{C}\otimes V_{\mathrm{dR}})\otimes(\mathbf{C}\otimes V_{\mathrm{B}})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}
is a non-degenerate pairing, and whose morphisms are the natural ones. The duality functor
V_{\mathrm{B}}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}V_{\mathrm{B}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}
induces an equivalence
T:\mathsf{Per}_{{\boldsymbol{K}},{\boldsymbol{k}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{Per}_{{\boldsymbol{K}},{\boldsymbol{k}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}
by means of the tautological pairing
({\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}):V_{\boldsymbol{k}}\otimes V_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\boldsymbol{k}}
.

Let us consider
(\mathscr{V},\nabla,\left\langle{{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}\right\rangle)
satisfying Assumptions
2.1
and
2.22
, and let us restrict to the case where
{\boldsymbol{K}}=\mathbf{C}
. It defines moderate and rapid decay period structures:

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V}),\mathrm{H}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}),\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{mod}}\bigr),

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V}),\mathrm{H}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}),\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{rd}}\bigr),

where
\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{mod}}:\mathrm{H}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}_{\boldsymbol{k}})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}^{r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V})
is composed from the natural isomorphism
\mathrm{H}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}_{\boldsymbol{k}})\otimes\mathbf{C}\simeq\mathrm{H}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}})
and the inverse of the de Rham isomorphism
\mathrm{H}^{r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{H}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}})
, and
\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{rd}}
is defined similarly.

Lemma 2.27
.

There is a natural isomorphism

\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V}),\mathrm{H}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}),\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{rd}}\bigr)\simeq\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{m-r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V}),\mathrm{H}^{m-r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}),\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{mod}}\bigr)^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}.

Proof.

This follows from the compatibility of the pairing
\mathsf{Q}
and the Poincaré-Verdier duality pairing via the de Rham isomorphism.
∎

Proposition 2.28
.

There are isomorphisms of
(\mathbf{C},{\boldsymbol{k}})
-period structures

\displaystyle T\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V}),\mathrm{H}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}),\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{mod}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\simeq\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V}),\mathrm{H}_{r}^{\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V})_{\boldsymbol{k}},\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{r}\bigr),

\displaystyle T\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V}),\mathrm{H}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}),\mathrm{comp}_{\mathrm{rd}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\simeq\bigl(\mathrm{H}^{r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}(M,\mathscr{V}),\mathrm{H}_{r}^{\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V})_{\boldsymbol{k}},\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{r}\bigr).

Proof.

We will check the first case for example. The pairing corresponding to the dual presentation of the left-hand side amounts, according to the Poincaré-Verdier duality pairing

\mathrm{H}^{m-r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}_{\boldsymbol{k}})\otimes\mathrm{H}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}_{\boldsymbol{k}})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\boldsymbol{k}},

to the period pairing

\mathsf{Q}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{m-r}:\mathrm{H}^{m-r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}_{\boldsymbol{k}})\otimes\mathrm{H}^{r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C},

which corresponds

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

either to the complexified Poincaré-Verdier pairing via the de Rham isomorphism

\mathrm{H}^{r}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{mod}}(M,\mathscr{V})\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\!\mathrm{H}^{r}(M,\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}),

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

or to the pairing
\mathsf{Q}^{m-r}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}
after complexifying the first term and applying the corresponding de Rham isomorphism.

Let us recall that the Poincaré isomorphism (
2.6
) is defined over
{\boldsymbol{k}}
(see (
2.21
)), so that, applying it to the first term, we obtain the period pairing
\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{r}
.
∎

2.f.
Quadratic relations for good meromorphic flat bundles

We now consider the complex setup. Let
X
be a compact complex manifold of complex dimension
n
, let
D
be a divisor with normal crossings, and let
(\mathscr{V},\nabla)
be a coherent
\mathscr{O}_{X}(*D)
-module with integrable connection, endowed with a non-degenerate flat pairing
\left\langle{{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}\right\rangle:\mathscr{V}\otimes\mathscr{V}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{O}_{X}(*D)
. Let
\varpi:\widetilde{X}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}X
denote the oriented real blow-up
of
X
along the irreducible components of
D
. In a local chart of
X
with coordinates
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
where
D
is defined by the equation
x_{1}\cdots x_{\ell}=0
,
\widetilde{X}
is the space of polar coordinates
(r_{1},e^{\mathsf{i}\theta_{1}},\dots,r_{\ell},e^{\mathsf{i}\theta_{\ell}},x_{\ell+1},\dots,x_{n})
and the
i
-th component of
\varpi
(
1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell
) sends
(r_{i},e^{\mathsf{i}\theta_{i}})
to
x_{i}=r_{i}\cdot e^{\mathsf{i}\theta_{i}}
(see e.g.
[
51
, §8.2]
for the global construction of
(\widetilde{X},\varpi)
). We set
\widetilde{D}=\varpi^{-1}(D)=\partial\widetilde{X}
, and
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})
will play the role of
(M,{\partial M})
in the settings
2.a
.

There is a natural Cauchy-Riemann operator acting on the sheaf of
C^{\infty}
functions on
\widetilde{X}
and one defines the sheaves
\mathscr{A}_{\widetilde{X}},\mathscr{A}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\mathrm{rd}},\mathscr{A}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\mathrm{mod}}
respectively as the subsheaves of
\mathscr{C}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\infty},\mathscr{C}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\infty,\mathrm{rd}},\mathscr{C}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\infty,\mathrm{mod}}
annihilated by the Cauchy-Riemann operator (see e.g.
[
50
]
). Setting
\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}=\mathscr{A}_{\widetilde{X}}\otimes_{\varpi^{-1}\mathscr{O}_{X}}\varpi^{-1}\mathscr{V}
, the connection
\nabla
lifts to a connection
\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\varpi^{-1}\Omega^{1}_{X}\otimes_{\varpi^{-1}\mathscr{O}_{X}}\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}
. There is thus the corresponding notion of holomorphic de Rham complexes with growth conditions, that we denote by
\DR^{\mathrm{rd}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{V}},\nabla)
and
\DR^{\mathrm{mod}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{V}},\nabla)
.

Remark 2.29
.

By the Dolbeault lemmas (see Appendix
B
), the complex
\DR^{\mathrm{rd}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{V}},\nabla)
is quasi-isomorphic to its
C^{\infty}
analogue, and the same property holds for the complex
\DR^{\mathrm{mod}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{V}},\nabla)
. Hence there is no risk of confusion in the notation. On the other hand, moderate and rapid decay de Rham hypercohomologies of
\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}
on
\widetilde{X}
can be identified with analytic de Rham hypercohomologies on
X
of
\mathscr{V}
and of the dual
\mathscr{D}_{X}
-module
\mathscr{V}(!D)
respectively (see
[
45
, Chap. 5]
). Under these identifications, the pairing
\widetilde{\mathsf{S}}^{r}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}
defined in Convention
2.31
below induces a pairing
\mathsf{S}^{r}
between
\mathrm{H}^{r}(X,\DR(\mathscr{V}(!D))
and
\mathrm{H}^{2n-r}(X,\DR(\mathscr{V}))
. Furthermore, if
X
is projective,
(\mathscr{V},\nabla,\langle{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\rangle)
is the analytification of an
\mathscr{O}_{X^{\mathrm{alg}}}(*D^{\mathrm{alg}})
-module with connection
(\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{alg}},\nabla^{\mathrm{alg}})
equipped with a non-degenerate pairing
\langle{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\rangle
(this follows from GAGA and the definition of a connection as in
[
15
, §I.2.3]
). Setting
U=X^{\mathrm{alg}}\smallsetminus\penalty D^{\mathrm{alg}}
and
V=\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{alg}}|_{U}
, then in each degree
r
the pairing
\mathsf{S}^{r}
is the analytification of a pairing (for which we use Zariski topology)

\mathsf{S}^{r}:\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}^{r}(U,V)\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}^{2n-r}(U,V)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C},

which is thus non-degenerate. In Section
3
, where
X
is algebraic of dimension one, we provide an algebraic computation of
\mathsf{S}^{1}
.

The goodness property

Let
x
be a point in
D
, and let
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
be local complex coordinates of
X
centered at
x
such that the germ
D_{x}
is given by
x_{1}\cdots x_{\ell}=0
(
\ell\leqslant n
). Let
\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{x}}
be the formal germ of
\mathscr{V}
at
x
, which is an
\mathscr{O}_{\widehat{x}}(*D)
-module (
\mathscr{O}_{\widehat{x}}(*D)=\mathbf{C}[\![x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]\!][1/x_{1}\cdots x_{\ell}]
) of finite type with the integrable connection induced by
\nabla
. We say that
(\mathscr{V},\nabla)
is
good
at
x
if

(a)

there exists a finite ramification

\rho_{x}:(x^{\prime}_{1},\dots,x^{\prime}_{\ell},x_{\ell+1},\dots,x_{n})\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}(x_{1}=x^{\prime p}_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}=x^{\prime p}_{\ell},x_{\ell+1},\dots,x_{n})

such that the pullback
\rho_{x}^{*}(\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{x}},\nabla)
decomposes as the direct sum of free
\mathscr{O}_{\widehat{x}^{\prime}}(*D^{\prime})
-modules of finite rank with an integrable connection of the form
\nabla={\mathrm{d}}\varphi+\nabla^{\mathrm{reg}}
, where
\nabla^{\mathrm{reg}}
has a regular singularities along
D^{\prime}=\rho_{x}^{-1}D
and
\varphi
belongs to a finite family
\Phi_{x}\subset\mathscr{O}_{X^{\prime},x}(*D^{\prime})/\mathscr{O}_{X^{\prime},x}
,

(b)

the finite family
\Phi_{x}
is
good
, in the sense that, for any
\varphi\neq\psi
in
\Phi_{x}\cup\{0\}
, the divisor of the meromorphic function
\varphi-\psi
is negative.
2
2

2

Goodness usually involves
\Phi_{x}
rather than
\Phi_{x}\cup\{0\}
. This stronger condition is needed for Assumptions
2.1
to be satisfied.

We say that
(\mathscr{V},\nabla)
is
good
if it is good at any
x\in X
. In particular,
\mathscr{V}
is locally
\mathscr{O}_{X}(*D)
-free. Two important results should be emphasized.

(1)

If
n=\dim X=1
, then any
(\mathscr{V},\nabla)
is good. This is known as the Levelt-Turrittin theorem, which has various different proofs (see e.g.
[
53
,
35
,
47
,
3
,
18
,
40
,
52
,
1
]
).

(2)

If
n\geqslant 2
, goodness may fail, but there exists a finite sequence of complex blow-ups over a sufficiently small analytic neighbourhood of
x
, or over
X
if
X
is quasi-projective, that are isomorphisms away from
D
and such that the pullback of
(\mathscr{V},\nabla)
is good along the pullback of
D
, which can be made with normal crossings. This is the Kedlaya-Mochizuki theorem (see
[
50
]
for
n=2
and rank
\leqslant 5
,
[
43
]
for
n=2
in the projective case,
[
29
]
for
n=2
in the local formal and analytic cases,
[
44
]
for
n\geqslant 2
in the projective case,
[
30
]
for
n\geqslant 2
in the local formal and analytic cases, and also in the projective case).

Theorem 2.30
.

If
(\mathscr{V},\nabla)
is good, then Assumptions
2.1
and
A.18
hold for the rapid decay and moderate growth de Rham complexes of
(\widetilde{\mathscr{V}},\nabla)
.

Convention 2.31
.

In the case of complex manifolds, in order to make formulas independent of the choice of a square root
\mathsf{i}
of
-1
,
we replace integration
\int_{X}
of a form of maximal degree with the trace
\tr_{X}=(1/2\pi\mathsf{i})^{n}\cdot\int_{X}
. The global de Rham pairing
\mathsf{Q}^{r}_{\mathrm{mid}}
in degree
r
on
\widetilde{X}
will thus be replaced with the normalized de Rham pairing, e.g.

\widetilde{\mathsf{S}}^{r}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}=(1/2\pi\mathsf{i})^{n}\mathsf{Q}^{r}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}},

which pairs
\mathrm{H}^{r}(\widetilde{X},\DR^{\mathrm{rd}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}))
and
\mathrm{H}^{2n-r}(\widetilde{X},\DR^{\mathrm{mod}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}))
and is thus perfect. We define its “middle” version
\widetilde{\mathsf{S}}^{r}_{\mathrm{mid}}
as in Section
2.c
, that we can interpret in terms of hypercohomologies computed on
X
as in Remark
2.29
, in which case we denote it by
\mathsf{S}^{r}_{\mathrm{mid}}
.

Let us for example consider the quadratic relations in the middle dimension
n
if
\left\langle{{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}\right\rangle
is
\pm
-symmetric.

Corollary 2.32
.

Assume that
X
is compact of dimension
n
. The quadratic relations for the matrices of the Betti period pairings

\pm(-2\pi\mathsf{i})^{n}\,\mathsf{B}^{\mathrm{mid}}_{n}=\mathsf{P}^{{\mathrm{mid}}}_{n}\cdot(\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{mid}}^{n})^{-1}\cdot{}^{t}\mathsf{P}^{{\mathrm{mid}}}_{n}

hold for a good meromorphic flat bundle on
(X,D)
, and the Betti intersection pairing can be computed with a suitable simplicial decomposition of
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})
as in Proposition
2.25
.

Proof of Theorem
2.30
.

Let us start with Assumption
2.1
(
1
). In dimension one, the rapid decay case is the Hukuhara-Turrittin asymptotic expansion theorem (see
[
53
]
and
[
40
, App. 1]
). In dimension
\geqslant 2
, the proof is essentially a consequence of the work of Majima
[
36
]
on asymptotic analysis (see also
[
48
, App.]
,
[
44
, Chap. 20]
). The rapid decay case is proved in
[
48
]
and the moderate case in
[
24
, App.]
with the assumption
n\leqslant 2
, but the argument extends to any
n\geqslant 2
(see
[
25
, Prop. 1]
); both cases are also proved in
[
45
, Prop. 5.1.3]
. The proof that Assumption
2.1
(
2
) holds is obtained by means of the local description below (see e.g. the proof in dimension one of Proposition
3.17
).

Due to the aforementioned theorems, the local structure of
\mathscr{H}^{0}\DR^{\mathrm{rd}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{V}})=\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}
and of
\mathscr{H}^{0}\DR^{\mathrm{mod}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{V}})=\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}
is easy to understand. Let
\Delta(\varepsilon)
denote a disc of radius
\varepsilon
. Near
x\in D
as above, the real blow-up
\widetilde{X}
is diffeomorphic to the product
(S^{1})^{\ell}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{\ell}\times\Delta(\varepsilon)^{n-\ell}
for some
\varepsilon>0
. In this model, denoting by
(r_{1},\dots,r_{\ell})
the coordinates on
[0,\varepsilon)^{\ell}
,
\widetilde{D}
is described as
r_{1}\cdots r_{\ell}=0
, giving it the structure of a real analytic set. The structure of manifold with corners is then clear.

From the set
\Phi_{x}
of exponential factors defined after some ramification

\rho_{x}:(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})=(x^{\prime}_{1},\dots,x^{\prime}_{\ell},x_{\ell+1},\dots,x_{n})\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}(x^{\prime a_{1}}_{1},\dots,x^{\prime a_{\ell}}_{\ell},x_{\ell+1},\dots,x_{n}),

one constructs a local real semi-analytic stratification of
\widetilde{D}
: any meromorphic function
\varphi\neq 0
in
\Phi_{x}
can be written in the ramified coordinates
(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})

as

\varphi=u_{\varphi}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}x_{i}^{\prime-q_{i}(\varphi)}(1+h(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}))
,
with
q_{i}(\varphi)
non-negative integers,

u_{\varphi}=u_{\varphi}(x^{\prime\prime})
holomorphic and invertible, and
h(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})
holomorphic.
Then we can write
u_{\varphi}(x^{\prime\prime})=r_{\varphi}(x^{\prime\prime})e^{\mathsf{i}a_{\varphi}(x^{\prime\prime})}
, where
r_{\varphi},a_{\varphi}
are real analytic functions of their arguments, and

\varphi\circ\rho_{x}=r_{\varphi}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}r_{i}^{\prime-q_{i}(\varphi)}\cdot\exp\bigl[\mathsf{i}(a_{\varphi}-\textstyle\sum_{i}q_{i}(\varphi)\theta^{\prime}_{i})\bigr]\cdot(1+h(r^{\prime}e^{\mathsf{i}\theta^{\prime}},x^{\prime\prime})).

We consider the sets defined by

\cos\bigl(a_{\varphi}-\textstyle\sum_{i}q_{i}(\varphi)\theta^{\prime}_{i}\bigr)=0,

and their projections
S_{\varphi}
to
\widetilde{D}
, which are real semi-analytic subsets of
\widetilde{D}
(the multi-dimensional Stokes directions); the desired stratification is any real semi-analytic stratification compatible with all these subsets. We also denote by
S_{\varphi}^{\leqslant 0}
(
resp.

S_{\varphi}^{>0}
) the corresponding subsets defined with
\leqslant 0
(
resp.

>0
).

In the neighbourhood of any
\widetilde{x}\in\varpi^{-1}(x)
,
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}|_{\widetilde{D}}
decomposes as
\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{\varphi}\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}_{\varphi}
, where
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}_{\varphi}{}
is zero if
\varphi=0
and otherwise constant on
S_{\varphi}^{>0}
and zero on
S_{\varphi}^{\leqslant 0}
. A similar property holds for
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}|_{\widetilde{D}}
, with the only change that
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}_{\varphi}
is constant if
\varphi=0
. In particular,
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}
and
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}
are locally
\mathbf{R}
-constructible in the sense of
[
28
, Def. 8.4.3]
(we can use a local stratification as above to check local
\mathbf{R}
-constructibility).
In this local setting,
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}
and
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}
, together with any simplicial structure compatible with any real semi-analytic stratification with respect to which they are
\mathbf{R}
-constructible,
3
3

3

It is standard that such a simplicial structure exists, see e.g.
[
28
, Prop. 8.2.5]
.
satisfy thus Assumption
A.18
.

In order to conclude that Assumption
A.18
holds in the global setting, we note that the pair
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})
comes equipped with a real semi-analytic structure which induces the previous one in each local chart adapted to
D
, and that
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}
and
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}
are
\mathbf{R}
-constructible, since
\mathbf{R}
-constructibility is a local property on
\widetilde{D}
, according to
[
28
, Th. 8.4.2]
. Then the same conclusion as in the local setting holds.
∎

3.
Algebraic computation of de Rham duality in dimension one

In this section, we restrict to the case of complex dimension one and we make explicit in algebraic terms the results obtained in Section
2.f
.

3.a.
Setting, notation, and objectives

Let
X
be a connected smooth complex projective curve, let
D
be a
non-empty
finite set of points in
X
,
and let us define
j:U=X\smallsetminus D\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}X
as the complementary inclusion,
so that
U
is affine. Depending on the context, we work in the Zariski topology or the analytic topology on
U
and
X
, and hence
\mathscr{O}_{U}
will have the corresponding meaning. We denote by
\mathscr{O}_{\widehat{D}}
the structure sheaf of the formal neighbourhood of
D
in
X
, so that
\mathscr{O}_{\widehat{D}}=\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{x\in D}\mathscr{O}_{\widehat{x}}
and
\mathscr{O}_{\widehat{x}}\simeq\mathbf{C}[\![z]\!]
for some local coordinate
z
centered at
x
. We fix an affine neighbourhood
U_{D}
of
D
in
X
. We set
U_{D}^{\circ}=U_{D}\smallsetminus D=U\cap U_{D}
and we denote by
\iota_{D}
the restriction functor
\mathsf{Mod}(\mathscr{O}(U))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathsf{Mod}(\mathscr{O}(U_{D}^{\circ}))
attached to the inclusion
U_{D}^{\circ}\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}U
. We set
U_{\widehat{D}}
to be the formal completion of
U_{D}
at
D
and we define
U_{\widehat{D}}^{\circ}
by
\mathscr{O}(U_{\widehat{D}}^{\circ})=\mathscr{O}(U_{D}^{\circ})\otimes_{\mathscr{O}(U_{D})}\mathscr{O}(U_{\widehat{D}})
. We similarly denote by
\iota_{\widehat{D}}
the restriction functor
\mathsf{Mod}(\mathscr{O}(U))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathsf{Mod}(\mathscr{O}(U_{\widehat{D}}^{\circ}))
.

We consider a locally free
\mathscr{O}_{U}
-module
V
endowed with a connection
\nabla
together with the associated
\mathscr{O}_{X}(*D)
-module with connection
j_{*}V
(one also finds the notation
j_{+}V
in the literature) that we denote by
\mathscr{V}
.
It is thus a left module over the sheaf
\mathscr{D}_{X}
of algebraic differential operators on
X
, and thereby endowed with an action of meromorphic vector fields
\Theta_{X}(*D)
with poles along
D
.

On the other hand,
let
(V^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee},\nabla)
be the dual bundle with connection and set
\mathscr{V}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}=j_{*}V^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}
. The dual
\mathscr{D}_{X}
-module
\boldsymbol{D}(\mathscr{V}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})
is a holonomic
\mathscr{D}_{X}
-module that we denote by
\mathscr{V}(!D)
(one also finds the notation
j_{!}V
or
j_{\dagger}V
in the literature).
There exists a natural morphism
\mathscr{V}(!D)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{V}

whose kernel
K
and cokernel
C
are supported on
D
and whose image is denoted by
\mathscr{V}(!{*}D)
.

We deduce natural morphisms between the associated algebraic de Rham complexes

\DR\mathscr{V}(!D)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\DR\mathscr{V}(!{*}D)\quad\text{and}\quad\DR\mathscr{V}(!{*}D)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\DR\mathscr{V}.

We denote the hypercohomologies on
X
of
\DR\mathscr{V}(!D)
,
\DR\mathscr{V}(!{*}D)
, and
\DR\mathscr{V}
respectively by
\mathrm{H}^{*}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,V)
,
\mathrm{H}^{*}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(U,V)
, and
\mathrm{H}^{*}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(U,V)
.

We note that
\DR K
and
\DR C
have non-zero cohomology in degree one at most and are supported on
D
, whence exact sequences

\begin{gathered}\boldsymbol{H}^{1}(D,\DR K)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,V)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(U,V)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0\\
0\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(U,V)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(U,V)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{H}^{1}(D,\DR C).\end{gathered}

In particular, we obtain an identification

(3.1)

\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(U,V)=\image[\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,V)\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(U,V)].

Moreover, when working in the analytic topology, one can define the analytic de Rham complex
\DR^{\mathrm{an}}\mathscr{V}
, which has constructible cohomology. Since
X
is compact, it can be deduced from the GAGA theorem that the natural morphism
\DR\mathscr{V}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\DR^{\mathrm{an}}\mathscr{V}
induces an isomorphism in hypercohomology (and similarly for
\mathscr{V}(!D)
). This enables us to identify the algebraic de Rham cohomology with the analytic one. We will not use the exponent ‘
\mathrm{an}
’ when the context is clear and we will use this GAGA theorem without mentioning it explicitly.

If
(V,\nabla)=(\mathscr{O}_{U},{\mathrm{d}})
, one knows various forms of
\DR(\mathscr{O}_{X}(!D))
. Namely,
\DR(\mathscr{O}_{X}(!D))
is quasi-isomorphic to one of the following complexes:

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

\mathrm{Cone}\bigl(\DR\mathscr{O}_{X}(*D)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\iota_{D}\DR\mathscr{O}_{\widehat{D}}\bigr)[-1]
,

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

\mathscr{O}_{X}(-D)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Omega^{1}_{X}
,

and, for the sake of computing cohomology, one can replace these complexes with their analytic counterparts. Another form proves useful:
it is obtained by means of the rapid decay de Rham complex defined on the real-oriented blow-up
\widetilde{X}
of
X
at each point of
D
(the one-dimensional version of the rapid decay de Rham complex considered in Section
2.f
, see Section
3.d
for details).

The first goal of this section is to explain similar presentations for
\DR\mathscr{V}(!D)
and, correspondingly, the presentation of
\DR\mathscr{V}
in terms of the moderate de Rham complex on
\widetilde{X}
. Furthermore, we express the natural de Rham pairing between these complexes in terms of the analytic de Rham pairing
\widetilde{\mathsf{Q}}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}
on the real blow-up space
\widetilde{X}
at
D
. This approach is reminiscent of that of
[
45
]
in any dimension (see Cor. 5.2.7 from loc. cit. and Remark
2.29
).

Theorem
3.13
gives a residue formula for this de Rham pairing
\mathsf{S}
. It is a generalization to higher rank of a formula already obtained by Deligne
[
16
]
(see also
[
17
]
) in rank one.

Once this material is settled, we translate into this setting the general results on quadratic relations obtained in Section
2.f
, with the de Rham cohomology (
resp.
with compact support) instead of moderate (
resp.
rapid decay) cohomology. Also, we focus here our attention on the middle extension (co)homology.

3.b.
Čech computation of de Rham cohomologies

Recall that, since
U
is affine, the de Rham cohomology of
(V,\nabla)
is computed as the cohomology of the complex

\Gamma(U,V)\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\kern 4.0pt\nabla\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}\Gamma(U,\Omega^{1}_{U}\otimes V).

Hence, any element of
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(U,V)
is represented by an element of
\Gamma(U,\Omega^{1}_{U}\otimes V)
modulo the image of
\nabla
.

We will compute the de Rham cohomology
in terms of a Čech complex relative to the covering
(U_{D},U)
,
whose differential is denoted by
\delta
.
So for a sheaf
F
of
\mathscr{O}_{X}
-modules
and
(f,\varphi)\in F(U_{D})\oplus F(U)
,
we have
\delta(f,\varphi)=\iota_{D}\varphi-f\in F(U_{D}^{\circ})
. We will write
\mathscr{V}_{D}=\Gamma(U_{D},\mathscr{V})
and
implicitly identify
\Gamma(U_{D}^{\circ},\iota_{D}V)
with
\mathscr{V}_{D}
.

Replacing
X
with the formal neighbourhood
\widehat{D}
of
D
in
X
gives rise to
\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}=\mathscr{O}_{\widehat{D}}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}\mathscr{V}
, which is an
\mathscr{O}_{\widehat{D}}(*D)
-module with connection, and hence endowed with an action of the formal vector fields
\Theta_{\widehat{D}}
.

Proposition 3.2
.

The complex
\DR\mathscr{V}(!D)
is quasi-isomorphic
to the
(-1)
-shifted cone of the natural morphism
\DR\mathscr{V}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\DR\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}
,
that is, to the simple complex associated with the double complex

\textstyle{j_{*}V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\widehat{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\nabla}
\textstyle{\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\nabla}}
\textstyle{j_{*}(\Omega^{1}_{U}\otimes V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\widehat{D}}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{1}_{\widehat{D}}\otimes\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}.}

The complex
\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma(X,\DR\mathscr{V}(!D))

is quasi-isomorphic to the simple complex associated with the double complex

\textstyle{\Gamma(U,V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\widehat{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\nabla}
\textstyle{\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\nabla}}
\textstyle{\Gamma(U,\Omega^{1}_{U}\otimes V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\widehat{D}}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{1}_{\widehat{D}}\otimes\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}.}

Proof.

Any holonomic
\mathscr{D}_{X}
-module
N
is endowed with a canonical exhaustive increasing filtration by coherent
\mathscr{D}_{X}(\log D)
-submodules
N_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}
indexed by
\mathbf{Z}
, called the
Kashiwara-Malgrange filtration
(see e.g.
[
49
, Chap. I, Prop. 6.1.2]
) such that

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

N_{-k}=\mathscr{O}(-kD)N_{0}
for any
k\geqslant 0
, and
N_{k+1}=N_{k}+\Theta_{X}N_{k}
for any
k\geqslant 1
,

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

for each
k\in\mathbf{Z}
,
the eigenvalues of
\Res\nabla
on
N_{k}/N_{k-1}
at each point of
D
have their real part in
[k,k+1)
.

For each
k
, the connection
\nabla
defines a connection
\nabla:N_{k}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Omega^{1}_{X}\otimes N_{k+1}
. The above properties imply that, for
k\neq 0
, the induced morphism on graded spaces
\nabla:\gr_{k}N\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Omega^{1}_{X}\otimes\gr_{k+1}N
is bijective, so in particular the natural inclusion morphism is a quasi-isomorphism:

(3.3)

\{N_{0}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\kern 4.0pt\nabla\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}\Omega^{1}_{X}\otimes N_{1}\}\overset{\sim}{\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}}\DR N.

For example, the following also holds:

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

If
N_{|U}=V
, then the natural morphism
N_{k}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{V}_{k}
is an isomorphism for any
k\leqslant 0
.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

The formation of the Kashiwara-Malgrange filtration is compatible with tensoring with
\mathscr{O}_{D}
and
\mathscr{O}_{\widehat{D}}
and, for any
k
,
N/N_{k}\simeq N_{D}/N_{D,k}\simeq N_{\widehat{D}}/N_{\widehat{D},k}
.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

For
\mathscr{V}=j_{*}V
, we have
\mathscr{V}_{k}=\mathscr{O}(kD)\mathscr{V}_{0}
for any
k\in\mathbf{Z}
.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

Among all
N
’s such that
N_{|U}=V
, the
\mathscr{D}_{X}
-module
\mathscr{V}(!D)
is characterized by the property that
\nabla:\gr_{0}N\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Omega^{1}_{X}\otimes\gr_{1}N
is bijective.

By the third point, the left-hand complex in (
3.3
) for
\mathscr{V}
reads

\{\mathscr{V}_{0}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\kern 4.0pt\nabla\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}\Omega^{1}_{X}(D)\otimes\mathscr{V}_{0}\},

so (
3.3
) amounts to the quasi-isomorphism

\{\mathscr{V}_{0}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\kern 4.0pt\nabla\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}\Omega^{1}_{X}(D)\otimes\mathscr{V}_{0}\}\overset{\sim}{\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}}\DR\mathscr{V}.

On the other hand, the last point implies that the inclusion of complexes

\{\mathscr{V}(!D)_{-1}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\kern 4.0pt\nabla\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}\Omega^{1}_{X}\otimes\mathscr{V}(!D)_{0}\}\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}\DR\mathscr{V}(!D)

is a quasi-isomorphism. The left-hand complex reads
\{\mathscr{V}_{-1}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\kern 4.0pt\nabla\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}\Omega^{1}_{X}\otimes\mathscr{V}_{0}\}
according to the second point, so we obtain a quasi-isomorphism

\{\mathscr{V}_{-1}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\kern 4.0pt\nabla\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}\Omega^{1}_{X}(D)\otimes\mathscr{V}_{-1}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\DR\mathscr{V}(!D).

The Čech resolution of the complex on the left
is the simple complex associated with the double complex

\textstyle{\mathscr{V}_{D,-1}\oplus j_{*}V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\scriptstyle{\nabla}
\textstyle{\mathscr{V}_{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nabla}
\textstyle{\bigl(\Omega^{1}_{U_{D}}(D)\otimes\mathscr{V}_{D,-1}\bigr)\oplus j_{*}\bigl(\Omega^{1}_{U}\otimes V\bigr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{\Omega^{1}_{U_{D}}(D)\otimes\mathscr{V}_{D}.}

Since
\mathscr{V}_{D,-1}\subset\mathscr{V}_{D}
, this complex is quasi-isomorphic to

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern Reversing the reasoning, it is also isomorphic to the Čech complex

0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-68.8269pt\raise-32.41666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigl(\Omega^{1}_{\widehat{D}}(D)\otimes\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D},-1}\bigr)\oplus j_{*}\bigl(\Omega^{1}_{U}\otimes V\bigr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 76.00954pt\raise-26.9861pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 92.8269pt\raise-32.41666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 92.8269pt\raise-32.41666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Omega^{1}_{\widehat{D}}\otimes\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

We claim that the morphism
\nabla:\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D},-1}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Omega^{1}_{\widehat{D}}(D)\otimes\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D},-1}
is bijective. Indeed, recall that
\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}
has a canonical decomposition (see
[
40
, Th. (2.3), p. 51]
)

\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}=\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}^{\mathrm{reg}}\oplus\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}^{\mathrm{irr}}

into its regular and irregular components and that the Kashiwara-Malgrange filtration
\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
decomposes accordingly. Moreover, the filtration
\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\mathrm{irr}}
is constant and equal to
\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}^{\mathrm{irr}}
, and we know that

\widehat{\nabla}:\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}^{\mathrm{irr}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Omega^{1}_{\widehat{D}}\otimes\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}^{\mathrm{irr}}

is bijective (use for example ii) in loc. cit. with
M^{\prime}\!=\!\mathscr{O}_{\widehat{D}}
and
M^{\prime\prime}\!=\!\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}^{\mathrm{irr}}
). On the other hand, for the regular holonomic part,

\widehat{\nabla}:\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D},-1}^{\mathrm{reg}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Omega^{1}_{\widehat{D}}(D)\otimes\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D},-1}^{\mathrm{reg}}

is bijective: indeed, by the very definition of the Kashiwara-Malgrange filtration, this property holds true for the morphism induced by
\widehat{\nabla}
on
\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D},-1}/\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D},-1-k}
for each
k\geqslant 1
, hence on the projective limit; for
\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D},-1}^{\mathrm{reg}}
, this projective limit is isomorphic to
\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D},-1}^{\mathrm{reg}}
itself, as follows e.g. from
[
49
, Chap. I, Lem. 6.2.6]
.

It follows from this claim that
\DR\mathscr{V}(!D)
is quasi-isomorphic to the complex given in the proposition, and the statement for
\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma(X,\DR\mathscr{V}(!D))
results immediately.
∎

Corollary 3.4
.

The formal de Rham complex
\DR_{\widehat{D}}\mathscr{V}(!D)
is quasi-isomorphic to zero.

Proof.

Indeed,
\DR_{\widehat{D}}\mathscr{V}(!D)
is the simple complex
associated with the double complex

\DR_{\widehat{D}}\mathscr{V}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\kern 4.0pt\id\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\id}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}\DR_{\widehat{D}}\mathscr{V}
.
∎

Corollary 3.5
.

The de Rham cohomology space with compact support
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,V)
consists of pairs

(\widehat{m},\omega)\in\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}\oplus\Gamma(U,\Omega^{1}_{U}\otimes V)

satisfying
\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{m}=\iota_{\widehat{D}}\omega
, modulo pairs of the form
(\iota_{\widehat{D}}(v),\nabla v)
for
v\in\Gamma(U,V)
.∎

Remark 3.6
.

The natural morphism
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,V)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(U,V)
is described in terms of the previous representatives as
(\widehat{m},\omega)\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\omega
.

(1)
The image
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(U,V)
of this morphism (see (
3.1
)) consists of classes of sections
\omega\in\Gamma(U,V)
such that
\iota_{\widehat{D}}\omega\in\image(\widehat{\nabla}:\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}})
. Therefore, any family consisting of
\dim\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(U,V)
linearly independent classes
[\omega]
in
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(U,V)
for which there exists
\widehat{m}\in\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}
satisfying
\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{m}=\iota_{\widehat{D}}\omega
is a basis of
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(U,V)
.

(2)
The kernel of this morphism consists of pairs of the form
(\widehat{m},\nabla w)
for some
w\in\Gamma(U,V)
, modulo pairs
(\iota_{\widehat{D}}(v),\nabla v)
. Each class has thus a representative of the form
(\widehat{m},0)
with
\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{m}=0
, and there is a surjective morphism

\ker\bigl[\widehat{\nabla}:\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}\rightarrow\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}\bigr]\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\hskip-17.92523pt\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\ker\Bigl[\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,V)\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(U,V)\Bigr].

(3)
If
V
has no constant subbundle with connection (e.g. if
V
is irreducible and non-constant), then a representative
(\widehat{m},0)
with
\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{m}=0
is unique. We thus conclude in such a case that

\ker\Bigl[\widehat{\nabla}:\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}\rightarrow\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}\Bigr]\simeq\ker\Bigl[\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,V)\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(U,V)\Bigr].

(4)
With the assumption in (
3.6
), a basis of
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,V)
can thus be obtained as the union of the following sets:

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

a basis of
\ker\widehat{\nabla}
in
\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}
,

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

a family of
\dim\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(U,V)

representatives
(\widehat{m},\omega)
for which the classes of
\omega
in
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(U,V)
are linearly independent (and thus form a basis of
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(U,V)
).

Remark 3.7

(
C^{\infty}
computation)
.

We consider the sheaves

\mathscr{V}_{\infty}=\mathscr{C}_{X}^{\infty}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}\mathscr{V}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathscr{V}(!D)_{\infty}=\mathscr{C}_{X}^{\infty}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}\mathscr{V}(!D)

and the corresponding
C^{\infty}
de Rham complexes
\DR^{\infty}(\mathscr{V})
and
\DR^{\infty}(\mathscr{V}(!D))
with differential
\nabla+\overline{\partial}
.
These complexes are fine resolutions of

\DR^{\mathrm{an}}(\mathscr{V})
and
\DR^{\mathrm{an}}(\mathscr{V}(!D))
. We also consider the sheaves of
C^{\infty}
functions on
U
having respectively moderate growth and rapid decay at the points of
D
, defined in a way similar to that of Definition
B.1
.
The Dolbeault complex
(\mathscr{E}_{X}^{\mathrm{mod},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},\overline{\partial})
(see Appendix
B
) is a resolution of
\mathscr{O}_{X}(*D)
.
Hence, the complex

\DR^{\infty,\mathrm{mod}}(\mathscr{V})=(\mathscr{E}_{X}^{\mathrm{mod},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}\otimes\mathscr{V},\nabla+\overline{\partial})

is a fine resolution of
\DR^{\mathrm{an}}\mathscr{V}
. Therefore, there is an isomorphism

\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(U,V)\simeq\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma(X,\DR^{\infty,\mathrm{mod}}(\mathscr{V})))

induced by

\Gamma(U,\Omega^{1}_{U}\otimes V)=\Gamma(X,\Omega^{1}_{X}(*D)\otimes\mathscr{V})\ni\omega\\
\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\omega_{\infty}=\omega\in\Gamma(X,\mathscr{E}_{X}^{\mathrm{mod},(1,0)}\otimes\mathscr{V})\subset\Gamma(X,\mathscr{E}_{X}^{\mathrm{mod},1}\otimes\mathscr{V}).

On the other hand,
\DR^{\infty}(\mathscr{V}(!D))
is quasi-isomorphic to the double complex
\DR^{\infty}(\mathscr{V})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\DR^{\infty}_{\widehat{D}}(\mathscr{V})
. By Borel’s lemma (applied to the coefficients in a basis of
\mathscr{E}_{X}^{k}\otimes\mathscr{V}
), this morphism is termwise surjective, hence the double complex is isomorphic to the
C^{\infty}
rapid decay complex
\DR^{\infty,\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V})=(\mathscr{E}_{X}^{\mathrm{rd},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}\otimes\mathscr{V},\nabla+\overline{\partial})
.
Therefore, there is an isomorphism

(3.8)

\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,V)\simeq\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gamma(X,\DR^{\infty,\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V})))

described as follows. For a class
(\widehat{m},\omega)
with
\nabla\widehat{m}=\iota_{\widehat{D}}\omega
, let us choose
m_{\infty}\in\Gamma(X,\mathscr{V}_{\infty})
whose Taylor series at
D
is equal to
\widehat{m}
. As above, we also regard
\omega
as belonging to
\Gamma(X,\mathscr{E}_{X}^{\mathrm{mod},1}\otimes\mathscr{V})
and we denote it by
\omega_{\infty}
. Then

\eta_{\infty}=\omega_{\infty}-(\nabla+\overline{\partial})m_{\infty}

has rapid decay at
D
. Moreover, it is
(\nabla+\overline{\partial})
-closed. The image of the class of
(\widehat{m},\omega)
by (
3.8
) is the class of
\eta_{\infty}
.

Example 3.9

(The case
(V,\nabla)=(\mathscr{O}_{U},{\mathrm{d}})
)
.

We are interested in
\mathrm{H}^{2}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U)
. This space is identified with
\mathrm{H}^{2}(X,\DR\mathscr{O}_{X}(!D))
. By Proposition
3.2
applied to
V=\mathscr{O}_{U}
, we identify
\mathrm{H}^{2}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U)
with the quotient of
\Omega^{1}_{\widehat{D}}(*D)
by the subspace consisting of elements of the form
{\mathrm{d}}\widehat{\varphi}-\iota_{\widehat{D}}\eta
, with
\widehat{\varphi}\in\mathscr{O}_{\widehat{D}}(*D)
and
\eta\in\Omega^{1}(U)
. Let us make explicit the canonical isomorphism

\can:\mathrm{H}^{2}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{H}^{2}(\Gamma(X,\mathscr{E}_{X}^{\mathrm{rd},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}})).

For
\widehat{\mu}\in\Omega^{1}_{\widehat{D}}(*D)
, one can choose a lift
\mu_{\infty}\in\Gamma(X,\mathscr{E}_{X}^{1,0}(*D))
. Since
{\mathrm{d}}\widehat{\mu}=0
, the
2
-form
{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\infty}
has rapid decay. Then,
\can[\widehat{\mu}]
is the class of
-{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\infty}
.

On the one hand, let
\res_{D}:\mathrm{H}^{2}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}
be induced by

\sum_{x\in D}\res_{x}:\Omega^{1}_{\widehat{D}}(*D)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}.

On the other hand, we consider the trace morphism

\tr_{X}:\frac{1}{2\pi\mathsf{i}}\int_{X}\colon\mathrm{H}^{2}(\Gamma(X,\mathscr{E}_{X}^{\mathrm{rd},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}.

The following result is standard: the morphisms
\tr_{X}
and
\res_{D}
are isomorphisms and we have

(3.10)

\tr_{X}\circ\can=\res_{D}.

To see the latter equality, we write, for a union of discs
\Delta_{r}
of radius
r\!>\!0
centered at the points of
D
,

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathsf{i}}\int_{X}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\infty}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathsf{i}}\int_{X\smallsetminus\Delta_{r}}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\infty}-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathsf{i}}\int_{\Delta_{r}}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathsf{i}}\int_{\partial\Delta_{r}}\mu_{\infty}-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathsf{i}}\int_{\Delta_{r}}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\infty}.

We can choose
\mu_{\infty}
so that, for some
R>0
,
\mu_{\infty}=\widehat{\mu}^{<0}+\nu_{\infty}
, where
\widehat{\mu}^{<0}
is the polar part of
\widehat{\mu}
and
\nu_{\infty}
is
C^{\infty}
on
\Delta_{R}
.
Then, since
{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\infty}
has rapid decay, the above integral tends to
\res_{D}\widehat{\mu}

as
r\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0
.

3.c.
Pairings

Starting from the tautological pairing

({\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}):V\otimes V^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{O}_{U},

one defines a pairing

(\!({\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})\!):\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,V)\otimes\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(U,V^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}^{2}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U)

in the following way.
For representatives
(\widehat{m},\omega)\in\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}\oplus\Gamma(U,\Omega^{1}_{U}\otimes V)
and
\omega^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\in\Gamma(U,\Omega^{1}_{U}\otimes V^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})
(see Corollary
3.5
), we set

(\!((\widehat{m},\omega)|\omega^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})\!)=(\widehat{m}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\iota_{\widehat{D}}\omega^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})\mod\{{\mathrm{d}}\widehat{\varphi}-\iota_{\widehat{D}}\eta\}.

Indeed, let us check that the formula does not depend on the choice of representatives of the de Rham cohomology classes.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

If
\omega^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}=\nabla v^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}
, we have

(\widehat{m}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\iota_{\widehat{D}}\nabla v^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})={\mathrm{d}}(\widehat{m}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\iota_{\widehat{D}}v^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})-\iota_{\widehat{D}}(\omega\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muv^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}),

which is of the form
{\mathrm{d}}\widehat{\varphi}-\iota_{\widehat{D}}\eta
.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

Similarly, if
(\widehat{m},\omega)=(\iota_{\widehat{D}}(v),\nabla v)
, then
(\iota_{\widehat{D}}(v)\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\iota_{\widehat{D}}\omega^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})=\iota_{\widehat{D}}(v\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\omega^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})
.

The residue pairing
\mathsf{R}^{1}
is defined as the composition (see Example
3.9
for the isomorphism
\res_{D}
)

\mathsf{R}^{1}:\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,V)\otimes\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(U,V^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\kern 4.0pt(\!({\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})\!)\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\!({\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})\!)}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}\mathrm{H}^{2}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,\mathbf{C})\xrightarrow[\sim]{~\textstyle\res_{D}~}\mathbf{C}.

In other words, on representatives
(\widehat{m},\omega)
and
\omega^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}
it is expressed by summing up the residues:

\mathsf{R}^{1}\bigl((\widehat{m},\omega),\omega^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\bigr)=\res_{D}(\widehat{m}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\iota_{\widehat{D}}\omega^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})=\sum_{x\in D}\res_{x}(\widehat{m}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\iota_{\widehat{x}}\omega^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}).

On the other hand, by working with the
C^{\infty}
realizations of Remark
3.7
, we consider the de Rham pairing

(3.11)

\mathsf{Q}:\DR^{\infty,\mathrm{rd}}(\mathscr{V})\otimes\DR^{\infty,\mathrm{mod}}(\mathscr{V}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(\mathscr{E}_{X}^{\mathrm{rd},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},{\mathrm{d}}),

which gives rise at the cohomology level to the pairing

\mathsf{S}^{1}:\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,V)\otimes\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(U,V^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\kern 4.0pt\mathsf{Q}^{1}\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Q}^{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}\mathrm{H}^{2}(\Gamma(X,\mathscr{E}_{X}^{\mathrm{rd},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}))\xrightarrow[\sim]{~\tr_{U^{\mathrm{an}}}~}\mathbf{C}.

Proposition 3.12
.

The de Rham pairing
\mathsf{S}^{1}
and the residue pairing
\mathsf{R}^{1}
are equal.

Proof.

As explained in Remark
3.7
, the elements

(\widehat{m},\omega)\in\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}\oplus\Gamma(U,\Omega^{1}_{U}\otimes V)\quad\text{and}\quad\omega^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\in\Gamma(U,\Omega^{1}_{U}\otimes V^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})

are sent in a natural way, by choosing a
C^{\infty}
lift
m_{\infty}
of
\widehat{m}
, respectively to

(m_{\infty},\omega_{\infty})\in\Gamma(X,\mathscr{V}_{\infty})\oplus\Gamma(X,\mathscr{E}_{X}^{\mathrm{mod},(1,0)}\otimes V)\quad\text{and}\quad\omega_{\infty}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\in\Gamma(X,\mathscr{E}^{\mathrm{mod},(1,0)}\otimes\mathscr{V}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}),

and
\omega_{\infty},\omega_{\infty}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}
are
(\nabla+\overline{\partial})
-closed. Set
\eta_{\infty}=\omega_{\infty}-(\nabla+\overline{\partial})m_{\infty}
as before. By reasons of type we have

\mathsf{Q}(\eta_{\infty},\omega_{\infty}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})=\bigl(-(\nabla+\overline{\partial})m_{\infty}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\omega_{\infty}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\bigr)=-{\mathrm{d}}(m_{\infty}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\omega_{\infty}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}).

With the notation of Example
3.9
, this equality reads

\mathsf{Q}(\eta_{\infty},\omega_{\infty}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})=\can(\widehat{m}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\omega^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})=\can(\!((\widehat{m},\omega)\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\omega^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})\!).

The proposition now follows from (
3.10
).
∎

By working on the real blow-up space we will also show:

Theorem 3.13
.

The de Rham pairing
\mathsf{S}^{1}
is a perfect pairing.

In particular, the residue pairing
\mathsf{R}^{1}
is perfect. Before starting the proof of the theorem which will be done in Section
3.d
, let us state its “middle” consequence.

Corollary 3.14
.

The residue pairing
\mathsf{R}^{1}
vanishes on
\ker\widehat{\nabla}\otimes\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(U,V^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})
and induces a non-degenerate pairing

\mathsf{R}^{1}_{\mathrm{mid}}:\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(U,V)\otimes\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(U,V^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}.

Moreover, if
V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}V^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}
is a
\pm
-symmetric isomorphism, it induces a
\mp
-symmetric non-degenerate pairing

\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(U,V)\otimes\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(U,V)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}.

Proof of Corollary
3.14
.

If
\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{m}\!=\!0
and
\omega^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}
satisfies
\iota_{\widehat{D}}\omega^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\!=\!\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{m}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}
(see Remark
3.6
(
3.6
)), we have
