\res_{D}(\widehat{m}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\iota_{\widehat{D}}\omega^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})=\res_{D}{\mathrm{d}}(\widehat{m}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{m}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})=0,

hence the first assertion. That
\mathsf{R}^{1}_{\mathrm{mid}}
is non-degenerate follows from the same property for
\mathsf{R}^{1}
. Assume now that
\langle{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\rangle
is a
\pm
-symmetric pairing on
V
and let
\omega,\omega^{\prime}
represent classes in
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(U,V)
, so that there exist
\widehat{m},\widehat{m}^{\prime}
such that
\nabla\widehat{m}=\iota_{\widehat{D}}\omega
and
\nabla\widehat{m}^{\prime}=\iota_{\widehat{D}}\omega^{\prime}
. Then

\displaystyle\res_{D}\langle\widehat{m},\iota_{\widehat{D}}\omega^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\res_{D}\langle\widehat{m},\nabla\widehat{m}^{\prime}\rangle=-\res_{D}\langle\nabla\widehat{m},\widehat{m}^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\mp\res_{D}\langle\widehat{m}^{\prime},\nabla\widehat{m}\rangle=\mp\res_{D}\langle\widehat{m}^{\prime},\iota_{\widehat{D}}\omega\rangle.\qed

Remark 3.15
.

Theorem
3.13
is of course a variant of the theorem asserting compatibility between duality of
\mathscr{D}
-modules and proper push-forward (see
[
42
]
), but more in the spirit of
[
45
, Cor. 5.2.7]
. The presentation given here owes much to
[
40
, App. 2]
. Nevertheless, the present formulation is more precise and can lead to explicit computations.

In the present form, this theorem has a long history, starting (as far as we know) with
[
16
]
(see also
[
17
]
) in rank one. The computation that we perform with Čech complexes by using a formal neighbourhood of
D
is inspired from loc. cit. The result was used by Deligne for showing compatibility between duality and the irregular Hodge filtration introduced in loc. cit.

In a series of papers mentioned in the introduction, Matsumoto et al. also gave duality results of this kind, either in the case of regular singularities or in rank one cases with irregular singularities (possibly in higher dimensions). An instance of Proposition
3.12
was already obtained in
[
41
]
.

3.d.
Computations on the real blow-up space

In this section, we revisit the results of Section
2.f
in the simpler case where
\dim X=1
and we give details on the proof that Assumption
2.1
(
2
) holds for
\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}
with respect to the tautological pairing
({\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})
.
In this section, we use the analytic topology on
U
and
X
.

Let
\varpi:\widetilde{X}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}X
denote the real oriented blow-up of
X
at each point of
D
. Recall that
\widetilde{X}
is endowed with holomorphic and
C^{\infty}
sheaves of functions with growth conditions. Working on
\widetilde{X}
makes proofs of local duality easier since all involved de Rham complexes are sheaves, i.e., have cohomology in degree zero only. We denote by
\widetilde{\jmath}:U\!\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}\!\widetilde{X}
the open inclusion and by
{\widetilde{\imath}}:\varpi^{-1}(D)\!\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}\!\widetilde{X}
the complementary inclusion.

For
\mathscr{V}
as in Section
3.a
, we set (see Section
2.f
for
\mathscr{A}_{\widetilde{X}}
)

\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}=\mathscr{A}_{\widetilde{X}}\otimes_{\varpi^{-1}\mathscr{O}_{X}}\varpi^{-1}\mathscr{V}=\mathscr{A}_{\widetilde{X}}(*D)\otimes_{\varpi^{-1}\mathscr{O}_{X}}\varpi^{-1}\mathscr{V}

(the second equality holds since
\mathscr{V}
is an
\mathscr{O}_{X}(*D)
-module), which is a locally free
\mathscr{A}_{\widetilde{X}}(*D)
-module of finite rank. The
C^{\infty}
de Rham complexes with growth conditions
\DR^{\infty,\mathrm{rd}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{V}})
and
\DR^{\infty,\mathrm{mod}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{V}})
have been defined in Section
2.f
and satisfy Assumptions
2.1
(
1
) (with respect to the tautological pairing
({\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})
) and
A.18
, according to Theorem
2.30
. We will check in Proposition
3.17
, as asserted in Theorem
2.30
, that they also satisfy Assumption
2.1
(
2
) with respect to the tautological pairing
({\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})
on
V\otimes V^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}
(as already mentioned, the results of Section
2.b
can easily be adapted to this setting).

The pushforwards by
\varpi
of these
C^{\infty}
de Rham complexes are isomorphic to the corresponding complexes on
X
, allowing for a computation on
\widetilde{X}
of the various algebraic de Rham cohomologies, according to Remark
3.7
.

Corollary 3.16
.

We have natural isomorphisms

∎

\mathrm{H}^{k}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(U,V)\simeq\mathrm{H}^{k}(\widetilde{X},\DR^{\infty,\mathrm{mod}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{V}})),\quad\mathrm{H}^{k}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,V)\simeq\mathrm{H}^{k}(\widetilde{X},\DR^{\infty,\mathrm{rd}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{V}})).

Recall that we set
\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}^{\mathrm{rd}}=\mathscr{H}^{0}\DR^{\infty,\mathrm{rd}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{V}})
and similarly with
\mathrm{mod}
.

Proposition 3.17
.

The natural pairing

\widetilde{\mathsf{Q}}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}^{\infty}:\DR^{\infty,\mathrm{rd}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{V}})\otimes\DR^{\infty,\mathrm{mod}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(\mathscr{E}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\mathrm{rd},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},{\mathrm{d}})\simeq\widetilde{\jmath}_{!}\mathbf{C}_{U}

is perfect, that is, the pairing induced on the
\mathscr{H}^{0}
is perfect:

\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}^{\mathrm{rd}}\otimes\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\mathrm{mod}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\widetilde{\jmath}_{!}\mathbf{C}_{U}.

Proof.

As the statement is local on
\widetilde{X}
, we work on an analytic neighbourhood
\Delta
of
x\in D
with coordinate
z
. The Levelt-Turrittin decomposition (see (
1
) in Section
2.f
) can be lifted locally on
S^{1}\times\{0\}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{A}_{\widetilde{\Delta}}
, so that the ramification
\rho
can be neglected by considering a local determination of
z^{1/r}
and
\log z
, where
r
is the order of ramification. We are in this way reduced to proving the assertions in the case of an elementary model entering the Levelt-Turrittin decomposition.

Let
\theta_{o}\in S^{1}
and let
\mathrm{nb}(\theta_{o})=(\theta_{o}-\delta,\theta_{o}+\delta)\times[0,\varepsilon)
be an open neighbourhood of
\theta_{o}
in
\widetilde{X}
. We set
\mathrm{nb}(\theta_{o})^{\circ}=\mathrm{nb}(\theta_{o})\cap U=(\theta_{o}-\delta,\theta_{o}+\delta)\times(0,\varepsilon)
and consider the decompositions of
\widetilde{\jmath}
as the composition of the open inclusions

\mathrm{nb}(\theta_{o})^{\circ}\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\kern 4.0pt\alpha^{+}\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha^{+}}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}}\mathrm{nb}(\theta_{o})^{\circ}\cup\bigl((\theta_{o},\theta_{o}+\delta)\times\{0\}\bigr)\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\kern 4.0pt\beta^{+}\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta^{+}}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}}\mathrm{nb}(\theta_{o})=\mathrm{nb}(\theta_{o})^{\circ}\cup\bigl((\theta_{o}-\delta,\theta_{o}+\delta)\times\{0\}\bigr)

and

\mathrm{nb}(\theta_{o})^{\circ}\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\kern 4.0pt\alpha^{-}\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha^{-}}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}}\mathrm{nb}(\theta_{o})^{\circ}\cup\bigl((\theta_{o}-\delta,\theta_{o})\times\{0\}\bigr)\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\kern 4.0pt\beta^{-}\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta^{-}}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}}\mathrm{nb}(\theta_{o}).

For an elementary model, three kinds of cases can occur for
\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}^{\mathrm{rd}}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\mathrm{mod}}
on such a sufficiently small neighbourhood:

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}^{\mathrm{rd}}=\widetilde{\jmath}_{*}V^{\nabla}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\mathrm{mod}}=\widetilde{\jmath}_{!}V^{{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\nabla}
,

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}^{\mathrm{rd}}=\widetilde{\jmath}_{!}V^{\nabla}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\mathrm{mod}}=\widetilde{\jmath}_{*}V^{{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\nabla}
,

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}^{\mathrm{rd}}=\beta^{+}_{!}\alpha^{+}_{*}V^{\nabla}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\mathrm{mod}}=\beta^{-}_{!}\alpha^{-}_{*}V^{{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\nabla}
.

Perfectness being clear in the first two cases, let us analyze the third one. Poincaré-Verdier duality shows perfectness of the natural pairing

(\beta^{-}_{*}\alpha^{-}_{!}V^{\nabla})\otimes(\beta^{-}_{!}\alpha^{-}_{*}V^{{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\nabla})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\widetilde{\jmath}_{!}\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{nb}(\theta_{o})^{\circ}},

so one only needs to show the equality of subsheaves of
\widetilde{\jmath}_{*}V^{\nabla}
:

\beta^{-}_{*}\alpha^{-}_{!}V^{\nabla}=\beta^{+}_{!}\alpha^{+}_{*}V^{\nabla}.

These subsheaves clearly coincide away from
\theta_{o}
, and both are zero at
\theta_{o}
, hence they coincide.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.13
.

By applying
\boldsymbol{R}\varpi_{*}
(which is
\varpi_{*}
here), the pairing
\widetilde{\mathsf{Q}}_{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}^{\infty}
induces the pairing
\mathsf{Q}
of (
3.11
). From the compatibility between Verdier duality and proper pushforward by
\varpi
, together with Proposition
C.4
, we deduce that
\mathsf{S}^{1}
is non-degenerate.
∎

3.e.
Computation of Betti period pairings

The formula for computing the period pairing

\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{1}\colon\mathrm{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{rd}}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathscr{V}})\otimes\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{mod}}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}

(see Section
2.d
) is the natural one described as follows. Let
\sigma\in\mathrm{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{rd}}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathscr{V}})

be represented by a finite sum
\sum_{\ell}c_{\ell}\otimes v_{\ell}
of twisted cycles where each
c_{\ell}\colon[0,1]\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\widetilde{X}

is a piecewise smooth simplex satisfying
c_{\ell}((0,1))\subset U

and
v_{\ell}
is a horizontal section of
\mathscr{A}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\mathrm{rd}}\otimes\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}

on a neighbourhood of the support of
c_{\ell}
.
(The boundary
c_{\ell}(o)
,
o\in\{0,1\}
, may lie in
\widetilde{D}
.) Let
\omega^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\in\Gamma(U,\Omega^{1}_{U}\otimes V^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})
be a representative of a de Rham class in
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{mod}}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})=\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(U,V^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})
.
Then

\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{rd},\mathrm{mod}}_{1}(\sigma,\omega^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})=\sum_{\ell}\int_{0}^{1}\hskip-5.69054ptc_{\ell}^{*}((v\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\omega^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})).

We will make explicit the formula for computing the period pairing

\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{1}:\mathrm{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{mod}}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathscr{V}})\otimes\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}

starting from a representative
(\widehat{m},\omega)
of a de Rham class in
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{rd}}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})=\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,V^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})
. With respect to the above formula, there is a supplementary regularization procedure to be performed.

Proposition 3.18
.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

Let
\sigma\in\mathrm{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{mod}}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathscr{V}})

be represented by a finite sum
\sum_{\ell}c_{\ell}\otimes v_{\ell}
of twisted cycles
where each
c_{\ell}\colon[0,1]\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\widetilde{X}

is a piecewise smooth simplex satisfying
c_{\ell}((0,1))\subset U

and
v_{\ell}
is a horizontal section of
\mathscr{A}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\mathrm{mod}}\otimes\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}

on a neighbourhood of the support of
c_{\ell}
.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

Let

(\widehat{m},\omega)\in\mathscr{V}_{\widehat{D}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\oplus\Gamma(U,\Omega^{1}_{U}\otimes V^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})

be a representative of
[(\widehat{m},\omega)]\in\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,V^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})
.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

For
o\in\{0,1\}
,
if
c_{\ell}(o)\in\widetilde{D}
,
let
\widetilde{m}_{\ell,o}
be a germ in
\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{c_{\ell}(o)}

having
\widehat{m}_{\ell,o}
as asymptotic expansion at
c_{\ell}(o)
.
Otherwise let
\widetilde{m}_{\ell,o}=0
.

Then the following equality holds:

\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{1}(\sigma,[(\widehat{m},\omega)])=\sum_{\ell}\lim_{\varepsilon\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0}\biggl[\biggl(\int_{\varepsilon}^{1-\varepsilon}\hskip-5.69054ptc_{\ell}^{*}((v\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\omega))\biggr)+(v\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{m}_{\ell,0})(c_{\ell}(\varepsilon))-(v\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{m}_{\ell,1})(c_{\ell}(1-\varepsilon))\biggr].

Remark 3.19
.

In practice, one only needs to know the first few terms of the asymptotic expansion of
\widetilde{m}_{\ell,o}
in order to compute the limit.
The knowledge of
\widehat{m}_{\ell,o}
up to a finite order is therefore enough.

Proof.

We start with the
C^{\infty}
setting.

Lemma 3.20
.

Let
\sigma
be as in the proposition and let
\eta_{\infty}=\omega_{\infty}-(\nabla+\overline{\partial})m_{\infty}
be a representative of a class in
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,V^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})
as in Remark
3.7
. Then

\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{1}(\sigma,[\eta_{\infty}])=\sum_{\ell}\lim_{\varepsilon\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0}\biggl[\biggl(\int_{\varepsilon}^{1-\varepsilon}\hskip-5.69054ptc_{\ell}^{*}((v\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\omega_{\infty}))\biggr)+(v\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mum_{\infty})(c_{\ell}(\varepsilon))-(v\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mum_{\infty})(c_{\ell}(1-\varepsilon))\biggr].

Proof.

Since
\eta_{\infty}
has rapid decay, we have

\mathsf{P}^{\mathrm{mod},\mathrm{rd}}_{1}(\sigma,[\eta_{\infty}])=\sum_{\ell}\int_{0}^{1}\hskip-5.69054ptc_{\ell}^{*}((v\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\eta_{\infty}))=\sum_{\ell}\lim_{\varepsilon\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0}\int_{\varepsilon}^{1-\varepsilon}\hskip-5.69054ptc_{\ell}^{*}((v\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\eta_{\infty})),

and we write

\displaystyle\int_{\varepsilon}^{1-\varepsilon}\hskip-5.69054ptc_{\ell}^{*}((v\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu-(\nabla+\overline{\partial})m_{\infty}))

\displaystyle=-\int_{\varepsilon}^{1-\varepsilon}\hskip-5.69054pt{\mathrm{d}}c_{\ell}^{*}((v\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mum_{\infty}))

\displaystyle=(v\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mum_{\infty})(c_{\ell}(\varepsilon))-(v\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mum_{\infty})(c_{\ell}(1-\varepsilon)).\qed

End of the proof of Proposition
3.18

Let
\widetilde{m}_{\ell,o}
be as in the proposition.
Then
\widetilde{m}_{\ell,o}-m_{\infty}
has rapid decay
at
c_{\ell}(0)
and
c_{\ell}(1)
along the path
c_{\ell}
,
so that we can replace
m_{\infty}
with
\widetilde{m}_{\ell,o}

in the formula of Lemma
3.20
without changing the limit.
∎

3.f.
Quadratic relations in dimension one

We summarize the consequences of the identifications previously obtained in this section to the form of quadratic relations in dimension one. The setting is as in Section
3.a
. We assume that
(V,\nabla)
is endowed with a non-degenerate pairing
\langle{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\rangle:(V,\nabla)\otimes(V,\nabla)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(\mathscr{O}_{U},{\mathrm{d}})
. We assume that it is symmetric or skew-symmetric, that we denote by
\pm
-symmetric. We will make explicit the way to express middle quadratic relations (Corollaries
2.32
,
2.16
, and Remark
2.17
) in the present setting.

Middle de Rham pairing

Let
d
be the dimension of

\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(U,V)=\image\bigl[\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(U,V)\!\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\!\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(U,V)\bigr].

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

We choose
d
elements
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{d}
in
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(U,V)
and we solve the equation
\nabla\widehat{m}_{j}=\iota_{\widehat{D}}\omega_{j}
for each
j
and at each point of
D
, with
\widehat{m}_{j}\in V_{\widehat{D}}
. We choose such solutions
\widehat{m}_{j}
.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

The matrix
\mathsf{S}^{1}_{\mathrm{mid}}
of the de Rham pairing with respect to these families has
(i,j)
-entries
\sum_{x\in D}\res_{x}\langle\widehat{m}_{i},\iota_{\widehat{D}}\omega_{j}\rangle
. It is
\mp
-symmetric.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

If
\det\mathsf{S}^{1}_{\mathrm{mid}}\neq 0
, then the family
(\omega_{j})
is a basis of
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(U,V)
.

Middle Betti pairing

The space
\mathrm{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{mid}}(U,V)=\image\bigl[\mathrm{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{rd}}(U,V)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{mod}}(U,V)\bigr]
has also dimension
d
.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

We choose
d
elements
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{d}
in
\mathrm{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{mod}}(U,V)
which are the images of elements
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{d}
in
\mathrm{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{rd}}(U,V)
.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

The matrix
\mathsf{B}_{1}^{\mathrm{mid}}
of the Betti pairing with respect to these families is computed for example by means of Proposition
2.25
. It is
\mp
-symmetric.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

If
\det\mathsf{B}_{1}^{\mathrm{mid}}\neq 0
, then the family
(\beta_{i})
is a basis of
\mathrm{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{mid}}(U,V)
.

Middle period pairing

Let us fix a triangulation of
(X,D)
that is induced by a triangulation of
\widetilde{X}
such that the
\mathbf{R}
-constructible sheaves
\mathscr{H}^{0}\DR^{\mathrm{rd}}\mathscr{V}
and
\mathscr{H}^{0}\DR^{\mathrm{mod}}\mathscr{V}
are constant on each open
1
-simplex (see Theorem
2.30
or the proof of Proposition
3.17
). We assume the simplices are given an orientation. Let us write each
\beta_{i}
as
\sum_{\ell}c_{\ell}\otimes v_{i,\ell}
, where
c_{\ell}:[0,1]\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}X
runs among the oriented
1
-simplices of the chosen triangulation, and
v_{i,\ell}
is a (possibly zero) section of
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{an}}
with moderate growth in the neighbourhood of
c_{\ell}
. The cycle condition reads:

\text{for each vertex }x\in U^{\mathrm{an}},\quad\sum_{\ell\mid c_{\ell}(1)=x}v_{i,\ell}(x)-\sum_{\ell\mid c_{\ell}(0)=x}v_{i,\ell}(x)=0.

The middle period matrix
\mathsf{P}^{1}_{\mathrm{mid}}
is the
d\times d
matrix with entries

\mathrm{Pf}\,\sum_{\ell}\int_{c_{\ell}}\langle v_{i,\ell},\omega_{j}\rangle,

where the “finite part”
\mathrm{Pf}
means that, equivalently,

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

either we replace
\beta_{i}
with
\alpha_{i}
that we realize as
\sum_{\ell}c_{\ell}\otimes v^{\prime}_{i,\ell}
, where
v^{\prime}_{i,\ell}
has rapid decay near the boundary points of
c_{\ell}
that are contained in
D
and satisfy the cycle condition, and

\mathrm{Pf}\int_{c_{\ell}}\langle v_{i,\ell},\omega_{j}\rangle=\int_{c_{\ell}}\langle v^{\prime}_{i,\ell},\omega_{j}\rangle,

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

or we replace
\omega_{j}
with
(\widehat{m}_{j},\omega_{j})
, for each
\ell
such that
c_{\ell}(o)\in D
(
o=0,1
), we choose a germ
\widetilde{m}_{j,\ell}^{o}
as in Proposition
3.18
, and we set

\mathrm{Pf}\int_{c_{\ell}}\langle v_{i,\ell},\omega_{j}\rangle=\lim_{\varepsilon\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0}\biggl[\int_{\varepsilon}^{1-\varepsilon}\hskip-11.38109ptc_{\ell}^{*}(\langle v_{i,\ell},\omega_{j}\rangle)+\langle v_{i,\ell},\widetilde{m}_{j,\ell}^{0}\rangle(c_{\ell}(0))-\langle v_{i,\ell},\widetilde{m}_{j,\ell}^{1}\rangle(c_{\ell}(1))\biggr].

Middle quadratic relations

They now read, in terms of matrices in the chosen bases (see Corollary
2.32
),

(3.21)

\mp(2\pi\mathsf{i})\,\mathsf{B}_{1}^{\mathrm{mid}}=\mathsf{P}_{1}^{\mathrm{mid}}\cdot(\mathsf{S}^{1}_{\mathrm{mid}})^{-1}\cdot{}^{t}\mathsf{P}_{1}^{\mathrm{mid}}.

Appendix A
Twisted singular chains

In this section, we recall classical results from the Cartan Seminars
[
7
,
8
]
. However, we do not use homology of cosheaves as in
[
6
]
. We assume that
X
is a compact topological space, so that all locally finite open coverings are finite. All sheaves are sheaves of vector spaces over some field
{\boldsymbol{k}}
, in order to avoid any problem with torsion.

A.a.
Presheaves and sheaves

Let
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}
be a presheaf on
X
and let
\mathscr{F}
be the associated sheaf. We have
\mathscr{F}_{x}=\varinjlim_{U\ni x}\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(U)
. For every open set
U\subset X
, there is a natural morphism
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(U)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\coprod_{x\in U}\mathscr{F}_{x}
.
We denote by
\widetilde{s}_{x}\in\mathscr{F}_{x}
the germ of a section
\widetilde{s}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(X)
. If
\widetilde{s}_{x}=0
, there exists
U_{x}
such that the image of
\widetilde{s}
in
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(U_{x})
vanishes. It follows that the support of
\widetilde{s}
, that is, the subset
\{x\in X\mid\widetilde{s}_{x}=0\}
is closed.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

We say that
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}
has the
surjectivity property
if

(A.1)

\text{for all }x\in X,\quad\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(X)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{F}_{x}\text{ \ is surjective}.

This property is also called
\Phi
-softness, for
\Phi
the family of closed subsets of
X
consisting of points only.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

We say that
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}
has the
injectivity property
if

(A.2)

\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(X)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\coprod_{x\in X}\mathscr{F}_{x}\text{ \ is injective}.

The latter condition is equivalent to asking that the natural morphism
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(X)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Gamma(X,\mathscr{F})
is injective, since (
A.2
) factorizes through the latter.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

We say that
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}
is a
fine presheaf
if, for any (locally) finite open covering
\mathscr{U}=(U_{i})
of
X
, there exist closed subsets
F_{i}\subset U_{i}
and endomorphisms
\widetilde{\ell}_{i}
of
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}
(i.e., each
\widetilde{\ell}_{i}
is a family, indexed by open sets
U
, of endomorphisms
\widetilde{\ell}_{i}:\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(U)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(U)
compatible with restrictions
U^{\prime}\subset U
in an obvious way) such that

(a)

\widetilde{\ell}_{i}=0
on
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(U)

if
U\cap F_{i}=\varnothing
,

(b)

for any open set
U
and each
\widetilde{s}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(U)
, the sum
\sum_{i}\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\widetilde{s})
exists in
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(U)
and is equal to
\widetilde{s}
. Let
\ell_{i}:\mathscr{F}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{F}
be the endomorphism associated with
\widetilde{\ell}_{i}
. If
x\notin F_{i}
, there exist
U\ni x
such that
U\cap F_{i}=\varnothing
, and hence
\ell_{i,x}=0:\mathscr{F}_{x}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{F}_{x}
. Moreover, for each
U
and
s\in\Gamma(U,\mathscr{F})
, we have, for any
x\in U
, the relation
\sum_{i}\ell_{i,x}(s_{x})=s_{x}
(the sum is finite since the covering is finite).

Proposition A.3
.

Assume that
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}
is a fine presheaf that satisfies the surjectivity property (
A.1
). Then the natural morphism

\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(X)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Gamma(X,\mathscr{F})

is onto. If it moreover satisfies the injectivity property (
A.2
), then it is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Once the first statement is proved, the second statement is obvious. Let
s\in\Gamma(X,\mathscr{F})
.
By (
A.1
), for any
y\in X
,
there exists a section
\widetilde{s}^{y}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(X)
whose germ at
y
is
s_{y}
.
Then there exists a neighbourhood
U_{y}
of
y

such that
\widetilde{s}^{y}_{x}=s_{x}
for any
x\in U_{y}
.
Let
\mathscr{U}
be a finite open covering of
X
such that each open set
U_{i}
is of the form
U_{y}
as above. Let
\widetilde{s}^{i}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(X)
be such that
\widetilde{s}^{i}_{x}=s_{x}
for any
x\in U_{i}
. On the one hand, we have
\ell_{i,x}(s_{x})=(\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\widetilde{s}^{i}))_{x}
for any
x\in U_{i}
, and on the other hand
s_{x}=\sum_{i\mid U_{i}\ni x}\ell_{i,x}(s_{x})
, whence

s_{x}=\sum_{i\mid U_{i}\ni x}(\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\widetilde{s}^{i}))_{x}=\sum_{i}(\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\widetilde{s}^{i}))_{x},

since
(\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\widetilde{s}^{i}))_{x}=0
for
x\notin U_{i}
according to (
a
) above. We conclude, since the covering is finite,

s=\sum_{i}\image(\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\widetilde{s}^{i}))=\image\Bigl(\sum_{i}\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\widetilde{s}^{i})\Bigr),

where we consider the image of the morphism
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(X)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Gamma(X,\mathscr{F})
. It follows that the natural morphism
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(X)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Gamma(X,\mathscr{F})
is surjective.
∎

A.b.
Homotopically fine sheaves

See
[
10
, §8]
.

Definition A.4
.

Let
(\widetilde{\mathscr{F}},{\mathrm{d}})
be a
\mathbf{Z}
-graded differential presheaf on
X
. We say that
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}
is homotopically fine if, for any (locally) finite open covering
\mathscr{U}=(U_{i})
of
X
, there exist closed subsets
F_{i}
contained in
U_{i}
and endomorphisms
\widetilde{\ell}_{i}
and
\widetilde{k}
of
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}
such that

(1)

\widetilde{\ell}_{i}=0:\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(U)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(U)
if
U\cap F_{i}=\varnothing
,

(2)

\sum_{i}\widetilde{\ell}_{i}=\id+\widetilde{k}{\mathrm{d}}+{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{k}
.

We have a similar definition for sheaves. We note the following properties:

(a)

If a graded differential presheaf is homotopically fine, the associated graded differential sheaf is also homotopically fine.

(b)

If
(\widetilde{\mathscr{F}},{\mathrm{d}})
is homotopically fine and if
\widetilde{\mathscr{G}}
is any presheaf, then the graded differential presheaf
(\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}\otimes\widetilde{\mathscr{G}},{\mathrm{d}}\otimes\id)
is also homotopically fine. Recall that
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}\otimes\widetilde{\mathscr{G}}
is the presheaf
U\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(U)\otimes\widetilde{\mathscr{G}}(U)
.
The same property holds for graded differential sheaves.

Theorem A.5

(
[
11
, §3, Corollaire]
)
.

Let
(\mathscr{F},{\mathrm{d}})
be a homotopically fine
\mathbf{Z}
-graded differential sheaf. For each
p
, the natural morphism

\mathrm{H}^{p}(\Gamma(X,\mathscr{F}),{\mathrm{d}})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{H}^{p}(X,(\mathscr{F},{\mathrm{d}}))

is an isomorphism.

The proof is obtained by choosing a resolution
C^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}
of
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{X}
by fine sheaves and realizing
\boldsymbol{H}^{p}(X,(\mathscr{F},{\mathrm{d}}))
as
\mathrm{H}^{p}(\Gamma(X,(C\otimes\mathscr{F})^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})
, where
(C\otimes\mathscr{F})^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}
stands for the simple complex associated with the double complex
C^{p}\otimes\mathscr{F}^{q}
. The assertion follows from the degeneration at
E_{1}
of the second spectral sequence, since the second filtration is regular. We can complete this theorem in terms of presheaves as follows.

Proposition A.6
.

Let
(\widetilde{\mathscr{F}},{\mathrm{d}})
be a homotopically fine
\mathbf{Z}
-graded differential presheaf satisfying (
A.1
) and (
A.2
). Then the natural morphism

\mathrm{H}^{p}(\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(X),{\mathrm{d}})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{H}^{p}(X,(\mathscr{F},{\mathrm{d}}))

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

According to Theorem
A.5
, it is enough to prove that
(\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(X),{\mathrm{d}})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(\Gamma(X,\mathscr{F}),{\mathrm{d}})
induces an isomorphism in cohomology.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

Let
s\in\Gamma(X,\mathscr{F})
be such that
{\mathrm{d}}s=0
, and let
\widetilde{s}^{i}
be as in the proof of Proposition
A.3
. Recall that
\ell_{i,x}(s_{x})=(\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\widetilde{s}^{i}))_{x}
for any
x\in U_{i}
. Besides,
s_{x}=\sum_{i\mid U_{i}\ni x}\ell_{i,x}(s_{x})-{\mathrm{d}}ks_{x}
since
s
is
{\mathrm{d}}
-closed. Hence

s_{x}+{\mathrm{d}}ks_{x}=\sum_{i\mid U_{i}\ni x}(\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\widetilde{s}^{i}))_{x}=\sum_{i}(\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\widetilde{s}^{i}))_{x},

since
(\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\widetilde{s}^{i}))_{x}=0
for
x\notin U_{i}
according to (
a
) above. Since the covering is finite, we get

s+{\mathrm{d}}ks=\sum_{i}\image(\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\widetilde{s}^{i}))=\image\Bigl(\sum_{i}\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\widetilde{s}^{i})\Bigr).

Since
{\mathrm{d}}(s-{\mathrm{d}}ks)=0
, (
A.2
) implies that
\sum_{i}\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\widetilde{s}^{i})
is closed, which gives the surjectivity of the cohomological map.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

Let
\widetilde{s}
be a
{\mathrm{d}}
-closed section of
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(X)
whose image
s\in\Gamma(X,\mathscr{F})
satisfies
s={\mathrm{d}}\sigma
for some
\sigma\in\Gamma(X,\mathscr{F})
. Up to replacing
\sigma
with
\sigma+({\mathrm{d}}k+k{\mathrm{d}})\sigma
(and
\widetilde{s},s
with
\widetilde{s}+{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{k}\widetilde{s}
,
s+{\mathrm{d}}ks
) we can assume that
\sigma
is the image of some
\widetilde{\sigma}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(X)
as argued above. The image of
\widetilde{s}-{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\sigma}
in
\Gamma(X,\mathscr{F})
is zero, and hence
\widetilde{s}={\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\sigma}
, according to (
A.2
). This proves the injectivity of the cohomological map.∎

A.c.
Homotopy operator for singular chains

See
[
9
, §3]
.

Let (
S_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(X),\partial)
be the complex of singular chains. Recall that
S_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(X)
is the infinite-dimensional vector space having as a basis the set of singular simplices (continuous maps from a simplex to
X
). The support of a singular simplex is its image, which is a compact subset of
X
. For a closed subset
Z\subset X
, we regard
S_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(Z)
as the subspace of
S_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(X)
generated by simplices with support in
Z
and
S_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(X)/S_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(Z)
as the subspace generated by simplices whose support is not contained in
Z
. For any (locally) finite open covering
\mathscr{U}=(U_{i})
, there exist (see loc. cit.) endomorphisms
\ell
and
k
of
S_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(X)
(where
\ell
preserves the grading and
k
has degree one) such that

(A.7)

\begin{cases}{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}\text{ for any simplex $\sigma\in S_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(X)$, $\ell(\sigma)$ is a sum of simplices,}\\
\hphantom{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\text{each one being contained in some~$U_{i}\cap|\sigma|$,}\\
{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}\text{ if $|\sigma|$ is contained in some $U_{i}$, then $\ell(\sigma)=\sigma$,}\\
{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}\text{ $\ell=\id+k\partial+\partial k$ (in particular, $\ell\partial=\partial\ell$).}\end{cases}

For
Z
closed in
X
, let
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)
be the chain presheaf

U\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(U)=S_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(X)/S_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}((X\smallsetminus U)\cup Z)

and let
(\eucal{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)
be the associated differential sheaf (with the usual convention
(\eucal{C}_{X,Z}^{-{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},{\mathrm{d}})=(\eucal{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)
, we obtain a
(-\mathbf{N})
-graded differential sheaf). For any
x\in X
, we have

\eucal{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},x}=S_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(X)/S_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}((X\smallsetminus\{x\})\cup Z).

More precisely,
\eucal{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},x}
is identified with the subspace of
S_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(X)
with basis consisting of those singular simplices whose support contains
x
and is not contained in
Z
. Since
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X)=S_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(X)/S_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(Z)
, properties (
A.1
) and (
A.2
) obviously hold.

Proposition A.8

(
[
10
, p. 8]
)
.

Given a finite open covering
\mathscr{U}
of
X
, there exist a closed covering
(F_{i})
with
F_{i}\subset U_{i}
for all
i
and endomorphisms
\widetilde{\ell}_{i}
and
\widetilde{k}
of the presheaf
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
such that

(1)

\widetilde{\ell}_{i}=0:\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(U)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(U)
if
U\cap F_{i}=\varnothing
, and in particular
\widetilde{\ell}_{i}
induces the zero map on each germ
\mathscr{F}_{x}
with
x\notin F_{i}
,

(2)

for any singular simplex
\sigma
, each simplex component
\tau
of
\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\sigma)
satisfies
|\tau|\subset F_{i}\cap|\sigma|
,

(3)

\sum_{i}\widetilde{\ell}_{i}=\id+\widetilde{k}{\mathrm{d}}+{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{k}
.

Corollary A.9
.

The presheaf
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)
is homotopically fine.∎

It follows that the chain complex
(\eucal{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)
is homotopically fine.

Proof of Proposition
A.8
.

Let
(U_{i})_{i\in I}
be a finite open covering of
X
. One can find closed subsets
F_{i}\subset U_{i}
such that
(F_{i})_{i\in I}
is a closed covering of
X
. We fix a total order on
I
and we write
I=\{1,\dots,n\}
. We also fix
\ell
and
k
as in (
A.7
).

Let
\sigma
be a simplex in
S_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(X)
. Then we define
\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\sigma)
inductively on
i=1,\dots,n
as the sum of components of the chain
\ell(\sigma)-\sum_{j<i}\widetilde{\ell}_{j}(\sigma)
whose underlying simplices have support in
F_{i}
. The support is also contained in
|\sigma|
. We can then extend the definition to any finite chain
\sigma\in S_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(X)
. Then
\ell(\sigma)=\sum_{i}\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\sigma)
in
S_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(X)
and
\sum_{i}\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\sigma)=\sigma+k\partial\sigma+\partial k\sigma
.

Let
U
be an open subset of
X
. Since
\ell,k,
and
\partial
preserve the support, the above construction also preserves
S((X\smallsetminus U)\cup Z)
, and thus defines endomorphisms
\widetilde{\ell}_{i}
and
\widetilde{k}
of
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(U)
satisfying the desired properties.
∎

A.d.
Singular chains with coefficients in a sheaf

We make a statement of
[
10
, p. 8]
precise. Let
\mathscr{F}
be any sheaf. We denote by
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{F})
the presheaf

U\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(U)\otimes\Gamma(U,\mathscr{F})

and by
\eucal{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{F})
the associated sheaf
\eucal{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\otimes\mathscr{F}
. By Corollary
A.9
and (
a
) and (
b
) after Definition
A.4
, both
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{F})
and
\eucal{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{F})
are homotopically fine. These are chain complexes indexed by
\mathbf{N}
, when equipped with the boundary
\partial\otimes\id
, that we simply denote by
\partial
. As usual, we regard them as differential sheaves with grading indexed by
-\mathbf{N}
by setting e.g.
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z}^{-p}(\mathscr{F})=\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,p}(\mathscr{F})
and with degree-one differential
{\mathrm{d}}
identified with
\partial
. Let us set, by definition,

\mathrm{H}_{p}(X,Z;\mathscr{F})=\boldsymbol{H}^{-p}(X,(\eucal{C}_{X,Z}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}(\mathscr{F}),{\mathrm{d}})).

Then, since each term of the complex
\eucal{C}_{X,Z}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}(\mathscr{F})
is homotopically fine, Theorem
A.5
implies

\mathrm{H}_{p}(X,Z;\mathscr{F})\simeq\mathrm{H}_{p}(\Gamma(X,(\eucal{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{F}),\partial)).

In particular, given an exact sequence
0\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{F}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0
of sheaves, there is a long exact sequence

(A.10)

\cdots\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{p}(X,Z;\mathscr{F}^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{p}(X,Z;\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{p}(X,Z;\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{p-1}(X,Z;\mathscr{F}^{\prime})\rightarrow\cdots

Besides, let us consider the vector space
4
4

4

It should not be confused with
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{F})(X)
, that is,
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X)\otimes\mathscr{F}(X)
.

(A.11)

\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X,\mathscr{F})=\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{\sigma}\bigl[{\boldsymbol{k}}\sigma\otimes\Gamma(|\sigma|,\mathscr{F})\bigr],

where the sum runs over singular simplices
\sigma
whose support is not contained in
Z
, together with the boundary map induced by
\partial\otimes\id
. More precisely, if
\partial\sigma=\sum_{k}\tau_{k}
, then
\partial(\sigma\otimes f)=\sum_{j}\tau_{j}\otimes f|_{|\tau_{j}|}
, where the sum is taken over
j
such that the support of
\tau_{j}
is not contained in
Z
. A
p
-cycle is a sum
\sum_{a}(\sigma_{a}\otimes f_{a})
such that, for each
(p-1)
-simplex
\tau
with support not contained in
Z
,

(A.12)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,k\\
\tau_{a,k}=\tau\end{subarray}}f_{a}|_{|\tau_{a,k}|}=0\in\Gamma(|\tau|,\mathscr{F}).

Such an element
\sigma\otimes f
, with
f\in\Gamma(|\sigma|,\mathscr{F})
, defines a global section in
\Gamma(X,\eucal{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{F}))
. Indeed, let
U
be an open neighbourhood of
|\sigma|
on which
f
is defined. Then
\sigma\otimes f
defines an element of
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(U)\otimes\Gamma(U,\mathscr{F})
, and hence of
\Gamma(U,\eucal{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{F}))
. Moreover, since the image of
\sigma
is zero in
\Gamma(U,\eucal{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})
if
U\cap|\sigma|=\varnothing
, this element is supported on
|\sigma|
, and hence extends in a unique way to a global section of
\eucal{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{F})
on
X
.

The natural morphism

(A.13)

\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X,\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Gamma(X,\eucal{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{F}))

is clearly compatible with
\partial
. Moreover, it is injective. Indeed, let
(\sigma_{k})_{k}
be distinct singular simplices and, for each
k
, let
f_{k}\in\Gamma(|\sigma_{k}|,\mathscr{F})
be such that
\sum_{k}\sigma_{k,x}\otimes\penalty f_{k,x}=0
for all
x
, where
\sigma_{k,x}
denotes the image of
\sigma_{k}
in
\eucal{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},x}
. Let
K_{x}
be the set of indices
k
such that
x\in|\sigma_{k}|
. Then
(\sigma_{k,x})_{k\in K_{x}}
is part of a basis of
\eucal{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},x}
, and
\sum_{k\in K_{x}}\sigma_{k,x}\otimes f_{k,x}=0
implies that
f_{k,x}=0
for any
k\in K_{x}
. Since
\mathscr{F}
is a sheaf, this implies the vanishing
f_{k}=0
in
\Gamma(|\sigma_{k}|,\mathscr{F})
for all
k
.

Proposition A.14
.

The chain map
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X,\mathscr{F}),\partial)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(\Gamma(X,\eucal{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\mathscr{F})),\partial)
induces an isomorphism

\mathrm{H}_{p}(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X,\mathscr{F}),\partial)\simeq\mathrm{H}_{p}(X,Z;\mathscr{F}).

Proof.

We argue as for Proposition
A.6
.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

Let
s\in\Gamma(X,\eucal{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\otimes\mathscr{F})
. There exists an open covering
\mathscr{U}
and
\widetilde{s}^{i}\in\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(U_{i})\otimes\mathscr{F}(U_{i})
such that
s_{x}=\widetilde{s}^{i}_{x}
for any
x\in\penalty U_{i}
. We decompose
\widetilde{s}^{i}=\sum_{j}\sigma_{ij}\otimes f_{ij}
, where
\sigma_{ij}
are singular simplices with support not contained in
(X\smallsetminus U_{i})\cup Z
, and
f_{ij}\in\mathscr{F}(U_{i})
. Let
(F_{i})
be a closed covering such that
F_{i}\subset U_{i}
for each
i
and let
(\widetilde{\ell}_{i},\widetilde{k})
be as in Proposition
A.8
, tensored with
\id
so that they act on the presheaf
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\otimes\mathscr{F}
.
We denote by
\ell_{i}
and
k
the induced sheaf morphisms.
We have
s=\sum_{i}\ell_{i}(s)-(\partial k+k\partial)s
. For any
x
,
\ell_{i}(s)_{x}=0
if
x\notin F_{i}
, according to Proposition
A.8
(
1
), and
\ell_{i}(s)_{x}=\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\widetilde{s}^{i})_{x}
otherwise, and therefore

\displaystyle s_{x}=\hskip-2.84526pt\sum_{i\mid U_{i}\ni x}\hskip-2.84526pt\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\widetilde{s}^{i})_{x}-((k\partial+\partial k)s)_{x}

\displaystyle=\hskip-2.84526pt\sum_{i\mid U_{i}\ni x}\sum_{j}\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\sigma_{ij})_{x}\otimes f_{ij,x}-((k\partial+\partial k)s)_{x}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\sigma_{ij})_{x}\otimes f_{ij,x}-((k\partial+\partial k)s)_{x},

since
\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\sigma_{ij})_{x}=0
if
x\notin U_{i}
. Moreover, if we write
\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\sigma_{ij})\otimes f_{ij}=\sum_{a}\sigma_{a}\otimes f_{a}
, where
\sigma_{a}
are singular simplices satisfying
|\sigma_{a}|\subset|\sigma_{ij}|\subset F_{i}
, and
f_{a}\in\mathscr{F}(U_{i})
, we have
(\sigma_{a}\otimes f_{a})_{x}=0
if
x\notin|\sigma_{a}|
, and hence we can replace
f_{a}
with its restriction in
\Gamma(|\sigma_{a}|,\mathscr{F})
. Therefore,

s=\image\Bigl[\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\sigma_{ij})\otimes f_{ij}\Bigr]-((k\partial+\partial k)s),

where the term between brackets belongs to

\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}(X,\mathscr{F})
.
If
s
is closed, then
s+\partial ks

is the image of
\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\widetilde{\ell}_{i}(\sigma_{ij})\otimes f_{ij}

and the latter is closed since
\partial(s+\partial ks)=0

and (
A.13
) is injective. This implies surjectivity of the homology map.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

Let
\widetilde{s}=\sum_{a}\sigma_{a}\otimes f_{a}
, where
\sigma_{a}
are singular simplices and
f_{a}\in\Gamma(|\sigma_{a}|,\mathscr{F})
, be closed and such that its image
s
is equal to
\partial s^{\prime}
. The above formula shows that, up to replacing
s^{\prime}
with
s^{\prime}+(k\partial+\partial k)s^{\prime}
(and
s
with
s+\partial ks
), we can assume that
s^{\prime}
lies in the image of
\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X,\mathscr{F})
. Then
\widetilde{s}-\partial\widetilde{s}^{\prime}
has image zero, and hence is zero according to the injectivity of (
A.13
), so the homology map is injective.∎

Proposition A.15

(Excision)
.

Let
Y
be a closed subset contained in the interior
Z^{\circ}
of
Z
. Then the natural morphism

\mathrm{H}_{p}(X\smallsetminus Y,Z\smallsetminus Y;\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}_{p}(X,Z;\mathscr{F})

is an isomorphism for all
p
.

Proof.

We consider the open covering
\mathscr{U}=(X\smallsetminus Y,Z^{\circ})
. Let
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\mathscr{U}}(X,\mathscr{F}),\partial)
be the subcomplex of
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X,\mathscr{F}),\partial)
for which the spaces
{\boldsymbol{k}}\sigma\otimes\Gamma(|\sigma|,\mathscr{F})
occurring as components are those for which
|\sigma|
is contained in one of the open sets of
\mathscr{U}
. By using
(\ell,k)
adapted to this covering (see (
A.7
)), one obtains that any closed
s\in\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,p}(X,\mathscr{F})
can be written as
\ell(s)+\partial ks
, thus showing the surjectivity of the homology map. On the other hand, if
s^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,p}^{\mathscr{U}}(X,\mathscr{F})
satisfies
s^{\prime}=\partial s
with
s\in\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,p+1}(X,\mathscr{F})
, we have
s^{\prime}=\ell(s^{\prime})=\ell(\partial s)=\partial\ell(s)
, hence the injectivity.
∎

Remark A.16
.

The long exact sequence (
A.10
) is not easily seen in the model
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X,\mathscr{F})
, while the excision property is better understood in that model, as well as the long exact sequence, for the closed inclusions
Z\subset Y\subset X
:

\cdots\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}_{p}(Y,Z;\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}_{p}(X,Z;\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}_{p}(X,Y;\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}_{p-1}(Y,Z;\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\cdots

On the other hand, if
\mathscr{F}
is the constant sheaf with fiber
F
(assume that
X
is connected), then for each singular simplex
\sigma
, the support
|\sigma|
is connected, so
\Gamma(|\sigma|,\mathscr{F})
is canonically identified with
F
by the restriction morphism

F=\Gamma(X,\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Gamma(|\sigma|,\mathscr{F}),

so

(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X,\mathscr{F}),\partial)\simeq(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X)\otimes F,\partial)

and
\mathrm{H}_{p}(X,Z;\mathscr{F})
is the usual singular homology with coefficients in
F
.

A.e.
Piecewise smooth and simplicial chains

Assume that
X
is a manifold (possibly with boundary). We denote by
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}^{\mathrm{sm}}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)
the subcomplex of
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)
consisting of piecewise smooth singular chains (i.e., having a basis formed of piecewise smooth singular simplices), and by
(\eucal{C}^{\mathrm{sm}}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)
the associated complex of sheaves. On the other hand, for a sheaf
\mathscr{F}
, we define
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}^{\mathrm{sm}}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X,\mathscr{F})
in a way similar to (
A.11
).

Proposition A.17
.

The inclusions of chain complexes

\displaystyle(\eucal{C}^{\mathrm{sm}}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}(\eucal{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial),

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}^{\mathrm{sm}}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X,\mathscr{F}),\partial)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X,\mathscr{F}),\partial)

induce isomorphisms in homology.

Proof.

We will prove the statement for the second inclusion, the proof for the first one being similar.

Let
s=\sum_{a}\sigma_{a}\otimes f_{a}
, with
f_{a}\in\Gamma(|\sigma_{a}|,\mathscr{F})
, be a
p
-cycle in
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X,\mathscr{F})
. We can assume that
f_{a}
is the germ along
|\sigma_{a}|
of
f_{a}\in\Gamma(U_{a},\mathscr{F})
for some open neighbourhood
U_{a}
of
|\sigma_{a}|
. The cycle condition is as in (
A.12
) and for each
\tau
there exists
U_{\tau}
such that the corresponding sum
\sum f_{a}
is zero on
U_{\tau}
. We can approximate each
\sigma_{a}
by piecewise smooth simplices with image contained in
U_{a}
and more precisely construct a family of approximations
\Sigma_{a}(t)=\sigma_{a}^{t}
(
t\in[0,1]
) with
\sigma_{a}^{0}=\sigma_{a}
and
\sigma_{a}^{t}
piecewise smooth for each
t\in(0,1]
such that
\tau\times[0,1]
has image in
U_{\tau}
for each face
\tau
of
\sigma_{a}
. We can then subdivide
\Delta_{p}\times[0,1]
to regard each
\Sigma_{a}
as a
(p+1)
-chain and the total family
S=\sum_{a}\Sigma_{a}\otimes f_{a}
as an element of
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,p+1}(X,\mathscr{F})
(we restrict
f_{a}
to
|\Sigma_{a}|\subset U_{a}
). The boundary chain
\partial S
reads

\sum_{a,k}T_{a,k}\otimes f_{a}|_{|T_{a,k}|}+\sigma_{a}^{1}\otimes f_{a}-\sigma_{a}^{0}\otimes f_{a},

where
T_{a,k}
is a map
\Delta_{p-1}\times[0,1]\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}U_{a}
satisfying
T_{a,k}^{0}=\tau_{a,k}
and
|T_{a,k}|\subset U_{\tau_{a,k}}
. The cycle condition on
s
implies that, for each
\tau:\Delta_{p-1}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}X
, we have

\sum_{a,k\mid\tau_{a,k}=\tau}f_{a}|_{U_{\tau}}=0,

so that
\partial S=\sigma_{a}^{1}\otimes f_{a}-\sigma_{a}^{0}\otimes f_{a}
. This shows the surjectivity of the homology map, and its injectivity follows.
∎

On the other hand, assume that
X
is endowed with a simplicial structure
\mathscr{T}
compatible with
Z
. By this, we mean that
X
is the support of a simplicial complex
\mathscr{T}
and
Z
is the support of a subcomplex
\mathscr{T}_{Z}
of
\mathscr{T}
. We denote by
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\triangle}_{\mathscr{T},\mathscr{T}_{Z},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)
the subcomplex of
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)
consisting of simplicial chains of the simplicial structure. We define correspondingly the simplicial chain complex
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\triangle}_{\mathscr{T},\mathscr{T}_{Z},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X,\mathscr{F}),\partial)
. We make the following assumption on
\mathscr{F}
and the simplicial structure.

Assumption A.18
.

For any simplex
\sigma
of the simplicial complex
\mathscr{T}
, there exists an open neighbourhood
U_{\sigma}
of
\sigma
that retracts onto
\sigma
and such that the sheaf
\mathscr{F}_{|U_{\sigma}}
can be decomposed as a direct sum of sheaves
\mathscr{F}_{\sigma,i}
, each of which is constant of finite rank on
U_{\sigma}\smallsetminus T_{\sigma,i}
and zero on
T_{\sigma,i}
, for some closed subset
T_{\sigma,i}
of
U_{\sigma}
that intersects
\sigma
along a (possibly empty or full) closed face
\tau_{i}
of
\sigma
.

We note that, if
\mathscr{T}^{\prime}
is a subdivision of
\mathscr{T}
and
\mathscr{T}^{\prime}_{Z}
is the corresponding subdivision of
\mathscr{T}^{\prime}_{Z}
, then
(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{T}^{\prime},\mathscr{T}^{\prime}_{Z})
satisfies Assumption
A.18
if
(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{T},\mathscr{T}_{Z})
does so.

Proposition A.19
.

Under Assumption
A.18
, the inclusion of chain complexes

(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\triangle}_{\mathscr{T},\mathscr{T}_{Z},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X,\mathscr{F}),\partial)\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X,\mathscr{F}),\partial)

induces an isomorphism in homology.

We immediately note the following consequence, which justifies that we denote from now on by
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\triangle}_{X,Z,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X,\mathscr{F}),\partial)
the complex
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\triangle}_{\mathscr{T},\mathscr{T}_{Z},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X,\mathscr{F}),\partial)
when Assumption
A.18
holds.

Corollary A.20
.

The homology of
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\triangle}_{\mathscr{T},\mathscr{T}_{Z},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X,\mathscr{F}),\partial)
is independent of
\mathscr{T}
provided Assumption
A.18
is satisfied. In particular, it is equal to the homology of
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\triangle}_{\mathscr{T}^{\prime},\mathscr{T}^{\prime}_{Z},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X,\mathscr{F}),\partial)
for any subdivision
\mathscr{T}^{\prime}
of
\mathscr{T}
.∎

Proof of Proposition
A.19
.

We can argue separately with
X
and
Z
and obtain the result for the pair
(X,Z)
due to compatibility between long exact sequences of pairs. So we argue with
X
, as in
[
46
, §34]
, by induction on the (finite) number of simplices in the simplicial decomposition
X
. Let
\sigma_{o}
be a simplex of maximal dimension (that we can assume
\geqslant 1
) and let
X^{\prime}
be the simplicial set
X
with
\sigma_{o}
deleted (but the boundary simplices of
\sigma_{o}
kept in
X^{\prime}
). The underlying topological space
X^{\prime}
is
X
with the interior of
\sigma_{o}
deleted. Then
(X^{\prime},\mathscr{F})
also satisfies Assumption
A.18
. We consider, in both simplicial and singular homology, the long exact sequences of pairs

\cdots\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}_{p}(\sigma_{o};\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}_{p}(X;\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}_{p}(X,\sigma_{o};\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}_{p-1}(\sigma_{o};\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\cdots

and the natural morphism between them. The assertion follows from Lemma
A.21
below.
∎

Lemma A.21
.

With the notation and assumption from Proposition
A.19
,

(1)

the natural morphism

(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\triangle}_{\sigma_{o},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\sigma_{o},\mathscr{F}),\partial)\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{\sigma_{o},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(\sigma_{o},\mathscr{F}),\partial)

induces an isomorphism in homology;

(2)

both natural inclusions

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\triangle}_{X^{\prime},\partial\sigma_{o},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X^{\prime},\mathscr{F}),\partial)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,\sigma_{o},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\triangle}(X,\mathscr{F}),\partial),

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X^{\prime},\partial\sigma_{o},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X^{\prime},\mathscr{F}),\partial)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,\sigma_{o},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(X,\mathscr{F}),\partial),

induce an isomorphism in homology.

Proof.

(
1
)
Let us decompose
\mathscr{F}_{|\sigma_{o}}
as in Assumption
A.18
, let us fix a corresponding component
\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{o},i}
of
\mathscr{F}
that we still call
\mathscr{F}
, and let
\tau_{o}
be the face of
\sigma_{o}
on which it is zero. The assertion is trivial if
\tau_{o}=\sigma_{o}
. If
\tau_{o}=\varnothing
, then
\mathscr{F}
is constant and the proof is done in
[
46
, §34]
.

Assume now that
\tau_{o}
is non-empty and different from
\sigma_{o}
. For any face
\tau
of
\sigma_{o}
intersecting
\tau_{o}
,
\mathscr{F}_{|\tau}
is constant on
\tau\smallsetminus(\tau\cap\tau_{o})
and zero on
\tau\cap\tau_{o}
, so that
\Gamma(\tau,\mathscr{F})=0
. Let
\tau^{\prime}_{o}
be the union of faces of
\sigma_{o}
not intersecting
\tau_{o}
. It is a single face of
\sigma_{o}
, and hence a simplex. Now,
\mathscr{F}
is constant on
\tau^{\prime}_{o}
and, obviously,
\mathrm{H}_{p}^{\scriptscriptstyle\triangle}(\sigma_{o};\mathscr{F})=\mathrm{H}_{p}^{\scriptscriptstyle\triangle}(\tau^{\prime}_{o};\mathscr{F})
.

Let us now compute
\mathrm{H}_{p}(\sigma_{o};\mathscr{F})
. Let
\sigma:\Delta_{p}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\sigma_{o}
be a singular
p
-simplex of
\sigma_{o}
, with image
|\sigma|
. Note that the natural morphism
\Gamma(|\sigma|,\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Gamma(\Delta_{p},\sigma^{-1}\mathscr{F})
is injective (since it preserves germs). If
|\sigma|\cap\tau_{o}\neq\varnothing
, the argument above shows that
\Gamma(\Delta_{p},\sigma^{-1}\mathscr{F})=0
, and hence so is
\Gamma(|\sigma|,\mathscr{F})
. Thus,
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{\sigma_{o},p}(\sigma_{o},\mathscr{F})
only involves singular simplices
\sigma
not intersecting
\tau_{o}
. Let
F
be the constant value of
\mathscr{F}
on
\sigma_{o}\smallsetminus\tau_{o}
. For such a simplex
\sigma
, we thus have
\Gamma(|\sigma|,\mathscr{F})\simeq\Gamma(\Delta_{p},\sigma^{-1}\mathscr{F})=F
, and then
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{\sigma_{o},p}(\sigma_{o},\mathscr{F})=\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{\sigma_{o}\smallsetminus\tau_{o},p}(\sigma_{o}\smallsetminus\tau_{o})\otimes F
. In other words,
\mathrm{H}_{p}(\sigma_{o};\mathscr{F})=\mathrm{H}_{p}(\sigma_{o}\smallsetminus\tau_{o})\otimes F
. Since
(\sigma_{o}\smallsetminus\tau_{o})
retracts to
\tau^{\prime}_{o}
, this group is also equal to
\mathrm{H}_{p}(\tau^{\prime}_{o};\mathscr{F})
, and we conclude with
[
46
, §34]
.

(
2
)
The assertion for the simplicial complexes is obvious since it is already an equality. We thus consider the singular chain complexes.

Let us prove surjectivity of the homology map. Let
s
be a closed singular chain in
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,\sigma_{o},p}(X,\mathscr{F})
. We can cover
X
by
U_{o}
and
U^{\prime}
, where
U_{o}=U_{\sigma_{o}}
is given by Assumption
A.18
and
U^{\prime}\subset X^{\prime}
. Choosing
(\ell,k)
as in (
A.7
), we write
s+\partial ks=s_{o}+s^{\prime}
, with
s_{o},s^{\prime}
closed in
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{U_{o},\sigma_{o},p}(X,\mathscr{F})
and in
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{U^{\prime},p}(X,\mathscr{F})\subset\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X^{\prime},\sigma_{o},p}(X^{\prime},\mathscr{F})
respectively. We decompose
s_{o}
according to Assumption
A.18
. For the component corresponding to “
\mathscr{F}
constant on
\sigma_{o}
”, it is proved in
[
46
, §34]
that it is homologous to a chain in
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{U_{o}\cap X^{\prime},\partial\sigma_{o},p}(X,\mathscr{F})
. Assume thus that
\mathscr{F}
is zero on
T_{o}
with
\tau_{o}=T_{o}\cap\sigma_{o}\neq\varnothing
, and constant on
U_{o}\smallsetminus T_{o}\neq\varnothing
. Arguing as in Case (
1
), we show that singular simplices occurring with a non-zero coefficient in
s_{o}
do not intersect
T_{o}
. There is a projection
\sigma_{o}\smallsetminus\tau_{o}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\tau^{\prime}_{o}
(with
\tau^{\prime}_{o}
as above),
so that if we compose
s_{o}
with the corresponding retraction,
we obtain a homologous closed
p
-chain in

\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{U_{o}\cap X^{\prime},\partial\sigma_{o},p}(X,\mathscr{F})
.

Assume now that
\widetilde{s}\!\in\!\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X^{\prime},\partial\sigma_{o},p}(X,\mathscr{F})
is equal to
\partial s
with
s
in
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,\sigma_{o},p+1}(X,\mathscr{F})
. It follows that
\partial\widetilde{s}=0
in
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X,\sigma_{o},p-1}(X,\mathscr{F})
, and hence in
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X^{\prime},\partial\sigma_{o},p-1}(X,\mathscr{F})
.
With
U_{o},U^{\prime}
, and
(\ell,k)
as above,
we have

\widetilde{s}+\partial k\widetilde{s}=\ell(\widetilde{s})=\partial\ell(s)=\partial s_{o}+\partial s^{\prime}.

The above argument shows that
s_{o}
is homologous to a
(p+1)
-chain in

\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{U_{o}\cap X^{\prime},\partial\sigma_{o},p+1}(X,\mathscr{F}),

and hence
\widetilde{s}
is the boundary of a chain in
\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{X^{\prime},\partial\sigma_{o},p+1}(X,\mathscr{F})
, which shows injectivity.
∎

A.f.
The dual chain complex with coefficient in a sheaf

In this section, we set
(X,Z)=(M,{\partial M})
, where
M
is a manifold with corners, and we consider a simplicial decomposition
\mathscr{T}
of
(M,{\partial M})
. Let
\mathscr{T}^{\prime}
denote the first barycentric subdivision of
\mathscr{T}
. For any simplex
\sigma
in
\mathscr{T}
, let
\widehat{\sigma}
denote its barycenter and let
D(\sigma)
be the open cell dual to
\sigma
, which is the sum of open simplices of
\mathscr{T}^{\prime}
having
\widehat{\sigma}
as their final vertex (see
[
46
, §64]
for definitions and details). If
\sigma
is not contained in
{\partial M}
, then the closure
\overline{D}(\sigma)
does not intersect
{\partial M}
. If
\sigma
is contained in
{\partial M}
, then
D(\sigma)
is contained in
M^{\circ}
. In any case, if we regard
\overline{D}(\sigma)
as a chain of
\mathscr{T}^{\prime}
relative to
{\partial M}
, then
\partial\overline{D}(\sigma)
is a sum of terms
\overline{D}(\sigma_{i})
for some
\sigma_{i}
in
\mathscr{T}
. The following is clear.

Lemma A.22
.

Let
\sigma
be a simplex of
\mathscr{T}
of dimension
p
. Then
\sigma
is the only simplex of dimension
p
of
\mathscr{T}
intersected by
\overline{D}(\sigma)
, and the intersection is transversal.

Figure 1.
Various examples of dual cells

We consider a sheaf
\mathscr{F}
on
M
satisfying Assumption
A.18
with respect to
\mathscr{T}
, with the supplementary condition that
\mathscr{F}
is locally constant on
M^{\circ}=M\smallsetminus{\partial M}
, that is, the closed subsets
T_{\sigma,i}
of Assumption
A.18
are contained in
{\partial M}
. We note that
\mathscr{F}
also satisfies the previous properties with respect to
\mathscr{T}^{\prime}
. The chain complex
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{\mathscr{T}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee},{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\square}(M,\mathscr{F}),\partial)
whose term of index
p
consists of the direct sum of the terms
\Gamma(\overline{D}(\sigma),\mathscr{F})
with
\sigma
of codimension
p
, is thus a subcomplex of
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{\mathscr{T}^{\prime},{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\triangle}(M,\mathscr{F}),\partial)
.

Lemma A.23
.

Under the previous assumptions, the inclusion of chain complexes

(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{\mathscr{T}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee},{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\square}(M,\mathscr{F}),\partial)\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{\mathscr{T}^{\prime},{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\triangle}(M,\mathscr{F}),\partial)

induces an isomorphism in homology.

Proof.

Similar to that of
[
46
, Th. 64.2]
.
∎

It follows from Proposition
A.19
that the chain complex
(\widetilde{\eucal}{C}_{\mathscr{T}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee},{\partial M},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\square}(M,\mathscr{F}),\partial)
also computes the relative homology
\mathrm{H}_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(M,{\partial M};\mathscr{F})
.

Appendix B
Poincaré lemma for currents

In this section, we work in a local setting. We let
U
be a convex open subset of
\mathbf{R}^{m}
(with coordinates
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
) containing the origin, we fix
r
such that
1\leqslant r\leqslant m
, we set
g(x)=\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{r}x_{i}
and

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\{x_{i}\geqslant 0\mid i=1,\dots,r\}\cap U,

\displaystyle\partial M

\displaystyle=\{g(x)=0\}\cap M,

\displaystyle M^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\{g(x)>0\}\cap M

\displaystyle U^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\{g(x)\neq 0\}.

A
C^{\infty}
function on
M
can be defined either as the restriction to
M
of a
C^{\infty}
function defined on some neighbourhood of
M
in
U
, or such that the derivatives to any order computed in
M
exist and are continuous in
M
. It is known that both definitions define the same sheaf of functions (e.g. by mirroring a function on
M
with respect to the hyperplanes
x_{i}=0
,
i=1,\dots,r
). The first definition means that the sheaf
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{M}
is the sheaf-theoretic restriction of
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{U}
.

B.a.
Functions with moderate growth and rapid decay along
{\partial M}

Recall that we work in a local setting made precise at the beginning of this section
B
.

Definition B.1
.

(1)

A
C^{\infty}
function
f
on
U^{\circ}
,
resp.
on
M^{\circ}
, has moderate growth along
g^{-1}(0)
,
resp.
along
{\partial M}
, if, for any relatively compact open subset
W\subset U
,
resp.

W\subset M
, and any multi-index
\alpha
, there exists
N\geqslant 0
and
C>0
such that
\partial^{\alpha}f_{|W^{\circ}}
is bounded by
C|g|^{-N}
.

(2)

A
C^{\infty}
function
f
on
U
,
resp.

M
, has rapid decay along
g^{-1}(0)
,
resp.
along
{\partial M}
, if all its derivatives vanish at any point of
g^{-1}(0)
,
resp.

{\partial M}
. Equivalently, the function
f/g^{N}
is
C^{\infty}
for every
N
. A
C^{\infty}
function
f
on
U^{\circ}
,
resp.

M^{\circ}
is said to have rapid decay along
g^{-1}(0)
,
resp.
along
{\partial M}
, if its extension by
0
to
U
,
resp.
to
M
, is
C^{\infty}
with rapid decay.

The corresponding sheaves are denoted respectively by
\mathscr{C}_{U}^{\infty,\mathrm{mod}}
,
\mathscr{C}_{U}^{\infty,\mathrm{rd}}
and
\mathscr{C}_{M}^{\infty,\mathrm{mod}}
,
\mathscr{C}_{M}^{\infty,\mathrm{rd}}
. Let
x_{o}
be a point in the intersection of exactly
\ell
smooth components of
g^{-1}(0)
. Then, in the neighbourhood of
x_{o}
,
U^{\circ}
has
2^{\ell}
connected components and
M^{\circ}
is one of them. A
C^{\infty}
function with moderate growth,
resp.
rapid decay, on such a neighbourhood is nothing but the family of its restrictions to the connected components, since these restrictions trivially glue together along
g^{-1}(0)
.

The corresponding de Rham complexes on
M
are denoted by
(\mathscr{E}_{M}^{\mathrm{mod},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},{\mathrm{d}})
and
(\mathscr{E}_{M}^{\mathrm{rd},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},{\mathrm{d}})
(we omit the boundary
{\partial M}
in the notation). On the other hand, we will also consider the sheaf of
C^{\infty}
functions on
M
with poles along
\partial M
, that we simply denote by
\mathscr{C}_{M}^{\infty}(*)
. It is a subsheaf of
\mathscr{C}_{M}^{\infty,\mathrm{mod}}
.

Proposition B.2

(Poincaré lemma)
.

The complexes
(\mathscr{E}_{M}^{\mathrm{mod},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},{\mathrm{d}})
and
(\mathscr{E}_{M}^{\mathrm{rd},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},{\mathrm{d}})
have cohomology in degree zero only, and are a resolution of
j_{*}\mathbf{C}_{M^{\circ}}=\mathbf{C}_{M}
and
j_{!}\mathbf{C}_{M^{\circ}}
respectively.

Proof.

We prove the statements on global sections. Let
\eta=\sum_{|I|=p}\eta_{I}{\mathrm{d}}x_{I}\in\mathscr{E}^{\mathrm{rd},p}(M)
(
resp.

\eta\in\mathscr{E}^{\mathrm{mod},p}(M^{\circ})
) be such that
{\mathrm{d}}\eta=0
and
p\geqslant 1
. There is an explicit formula (see for example
[
20
, (1.23)]
) computing
\psi\in\mathscr{E}^{p-1}(M)
such that
{\mathrm{d}}\psi=\eta
:

\psi(x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|I|=p\\
k\in[1,p]\end{subarray}}\biggl(\int_{0}^{1}t^{p-1}\eta_{I}(tx){\mathrm{d}}t\biggr)(-1)^{k-1}x_{i_{k}}{\mathrm{d}}x_{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{{\mathrm{d}}x_{i_{k}}}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathrm{d}}x_{i_{p}}.

This formula shows that
\psi
belongs to
\mathscr{E}^{\mathrm{rd},p-1}(M)
(
resp.
to
\mathscr{E}^{\mathrm{mod},p-1}(M^{\circ})
), hence the first assertion. The second assertion in both cases is clear.
∎

B.b.
Poincaré lemma for currents

The sheaf of distributions
\mathfrak{Db}_{M}
on
M
is defined as the sheaf-theoretic restriction to
M
of
\mathfrak{Db}_{U}
. As in
[
13
,
14
]
, we define the chain complex of currents
(\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)
. By definition, it is the sheaf-theoretic restriction to
M
of the complex
\mathfrak{C}_{U,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
that we consider now. A current
T
of dimension
q
on
U
can be paired with a test
q
-form
\varphi
on
U
(
C^{\infty}
with compact support) and the Stokes formula holds:
\langle\partial T,\varphi\rangle=\langle T,{\mathrm{d}}\varphi\rangle
. If
\omega
is a
C^{\infty}

(m-q)
-form, it defines a
q
-dimensional current by the formula
\langle T_{\omega},\varphi\rangle=\int_{U}\omega\wedge\varphi
. We can consider a current as a differential form with distributional coefficients, i.e., we can write
T=\sum_{\#J=m-q}\theta_{J}{\mathrm{d}}x_{J}
with
\theta_{J}\in\mathfrak{Db}(U)
. When considered as a form, the differential
{\mathrm{d}}
on currents extends that on
C^{\infty}
forms. The relation between
{\mathrm{d}}
and
\partial
is given, for a current of dimension
q
(i.e., degree
m-q
) by

\partial T_{q}=(-1)^{m-q+1}{\mathrm{d}}T_{q}.

For a tensor product taken over
\mathbf{C}
, like
(\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)\otimes(\mathscr{E}_{M}^{\mathrm{rd},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},{\mathrm{d}})
, the boundary is given by the formula, for a current
T_{q}\otimes\omega^{p}
(of dimension
q-p
):

(B.3)

\partial(T_{q}\otimes\omega^{p})=(-1)^{p}(\partial T_{q}\otimes\omega^{p}-T_{q}\otimes{\mathrm{d}}\omega^{p})=(-1)^{p}\partial T_{q}\otimes\omega^{p}+T_{q}\otimes\partial\omega^{p}.

Proposition B.4

(Poincaré lemma for
(\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)
)
.

The complex of currents on
M
satisfies

\mathscr{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }q\neq m,\\
\mathbf{C}_{M}&\text{if }q=m.\end{cases}

More precisely, the inclusion
(\mathscr{E}_{M}^{m-{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}{\mathrm{d}})\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}(\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

We prove the result on an open subset
U
of
\mathbf{R}^{m}
, and we obtain the proposition by sheaf-theoretic restriction to
M\cap U
. We refer to
[
20
, §2.D.4]
for the proof of the lemma below, originally in
[
13
,
14
]
. Let
T\in\mathfrak{C}_{q}(U)
.

Lemma B.5
.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, there exist
\mathbf{C}
-linear morphisms

R_{\varepsilon}:\mathfrak{C}_{q}(U)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathfrak{C}_{q}(U)\quad\text{and}\quad S_{\varepsilon}:\mathfrak{C}_{q}(U)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathfrak{C}_{q+1}(U)

such that

(1)

R_{\varepsilon}
takes values in
\mathscr{E}^{m-q}(U)
,

(2)

R_{\varepsilon}(T)-T=\partial S_{\varepsilon}(T)+S_{\varepsilon}(\partial T)
,

(3)

R_{\varepsilon}(\partial T)=\partial R_{\varepsilon}(T)
and
\lim_{\varepsilon\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0}R_{\varepsilon}(T)=T
weakly.

In particular,
R_{\varepsilon}
is a morphism of complexes
(\mathfrak{C}_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(U),\partial)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(\mathscr{E}^{m-{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}(U),{\mathrm{d}})
, and (
2
) implies that it is a quasi-isomorphism. Since
(\mathscr{E}^{m-{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}(U),{\mathrm{d}})
has homology concentrated in degree
{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}=m
, it follows that the same holds for
(\mathfrak{C}_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(U),\partial)
. Then we can sheafify the construction.
∎

Remark B.6
.

Arguing as in
[
14
, §15, Th. 12]
,
we can globalize the construction on a manifold with corners.
More precisely, given a covering
(U_{i})_{i\in I}
of
M
by charts, and
\varepsilon_{i}>0
small enough (
i\in I
), one can construct morphisms

R_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}:\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{E}_{M}^{m-{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}\quad\text{and}\quad S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}:\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}+1}

such that the conclusion of Lemma
B.5
holds for any
T\in\Gamma(M,\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})
, up to replacing
\varepsilon
with
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=(\varepsilon_{i})_{i\in I}
. In particular,
R_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}:(\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(\mathscr{E}_{M}^{m-{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},{\mathrm{d}})
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Let us end by recalling the computation of the Kronecker index made by de Rham. Let
T_{p}
and
T^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{m-p}
be two currents of complementary dimension on
U
. If both currents are closed, let us choose a decomposition
T_{p}=\Theta^{m-p}+\partial S_{p+1}
and
T^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{m-p}=\Theta^{{\scriptscriptstyle\vee},p}+\partial S^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{m-p+1}
, where
\Theta^{m-p}
and
\Theta^{{\scriptscriptstyle\vee},p}
are
C^{\infty}
closed forms. Then
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{dR}}(T_{p},T^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{m-p})
is by definition the integral
\mathsf{Q}(\Theta^{m-p},\Theta^{{\scriptscriptstyle\vee},p})
of the wedge product and is independent of the choices. Without the closedness assumption, but if one of the supports is compact, the intersection
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{dR}}(T_{p},T^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{m-p})
is defined by the limit, when it exists

\lim_{\varepsilon^{\prime},\varepsilon\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0}\mathsf{Q}(R_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(T_{p}),R_{\varepsilon}(T^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{m-p}))=\lim_{\varepsilon\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0}\langle T_{p},R_{\varepsilon}(T^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{m-p})\rangle.

We now consider the setting of the end of Section
A
. Let us choose a simplex
\sigma_{p}
in
M
not contained in
\partial M
and let
\overline{D}(\sigma_{p})
denote the dual cell in
M^{\circ}
, which intersects
\sigma_{p}
at its barycenter
\widehat{\sigma}_{p}
. Let us set
T_{p}=\int_{\sigma_{p}}
,
T_{m-p}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}=\int_{\overline{D}(\sigma_{p})}
and
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\sigma_{p},\overline{D}(\sigma_{p}))=\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{dR}}(T_{p},T^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{m-p})
.

Proposition B.7

(
[
14
, p. 85–86]
)
.

Under the previous assumptions, the intersection
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\sigma_{p},\overline{D}(\sigma_{p}))
exists and is given by the formula

\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\sigma_{p},\overline{D}(\sigma_{p}))=\pm 1,

where
\pm
is the orientation change between
\sigma_{p}\times\overline{D}(\sigma_{p})
and
M^{\circ}
.

Proof.

Let us choose coordinates
(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})=(x_{1},\dots,x_{p},x_{p+1},\dots,x_{m})
so that
\sigma_{p}
(with orientation) (
resp.

\overline{D}(\sigma_{p})
) is contained in the coordinate plane
(x_{1},\dots,x_{p})
(
resp.

(x_{p+1},\dots,x_{m})
). The formula for
R_{\varepsilon}
in Lemma
B.5
is:

\displaystyle R_{\varepsilon}(T_{m-p}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee})

\displaystyle=\theta_{m-p,\varepsilon}(x){\mathrm{d}}x_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathrm{d}}x_{p},

with

\displaystyle\theta_{m-p,\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\overline{D}(\sigma_{p})}\hskip-5.69054pt\frac{\chi_{\varepsilon}((x-y)/\psi(y))}{\psi(y)^{m}}\,{\mathrm{d}}y_{p+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathrm{d}}y_{m},

where
\chi:B(0,1)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{R}_{+}
is any
C^{\infty}
-function with compact support and integral equal to
1
and, for
\varepsilon>0
,
let
\chi_{\varepsilon}(v)=\varepsilon^{-m}\chi(v/\varepsilon)
with support in
B(0,\varepsilon)
. Hence, if we assume that
\chi
is even with respect to the variables
x^{\prime\prime}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\sigma_{p},\overline{D}(\sigma_{p}))

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0}\int_{\sigma_{p}}{\mathrm{d}}x_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathrm{d}}x_{p}\cdot\theta_{m-p,\varepsilon}(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0}\int_{\sigma_{p}\times\overline{D}(\sigma_{p})}\hskip-11.38109pt\frac{\chi_{\varepsilon}((x^{\prime},-x^{\prime\prime})/\psi(x^{\prime\prime}))}{\psi(x^{\prime\prime})^{m}}\,{\mathrm{d}}x_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathrm{d}}x_{m}=\pm 1

by the properties of
\chi
.
∎

B.c.
Poincaré lemma for current with moderate growth and rapid decay

Let us first consider the sheaf
\mathfrak{Db}^{\mathrm{mod}}_{U}
on
U
, defined as the subsheaf of
j_{*}\mathfrak{Db}_{U^{\circ}}
consisting of distributions extendable to
U
. Its space of sections on an open set
W\subset U
is the topological dual of the space of
C^{\infty}
functions on
W
with compact support and with rapid decay along
g^{-1}(0)
, endowed with its usual family of semi-norms. It follows from
[
39
, Chap. VII]
that
\mathfrak{Db}^{\mathrm{mod}}_{U}=\mathscr{C}_{U}^{\infty}(*)\otimes_{\mathscr{C}_{U}^{\infty}}\mathfrak{Db}_{U}
, where we have set
\mathscr{C}_{U}^{\infty}(*)=\mathscr{C}_{U}^{\infty}[1/g]
.
We also have an exact sequence

(B.8)

0\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathfrak{Db}_{[g^{-1}(0)]}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathfrak{Db}_{U}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathfrak{Db}_{U}^{\mathrm{mod}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0,

where
\mathfrak{Db}_{[g^{-1}(0)]}
denotes the subsheaf of distributions supported on
g^{-1}(0)
.

The sheaf
\mathfrak{Db}^{\mathrm{mod}}_{M}
is defined as the subsheaf of
j_{*}\mathfrak{Db}_{M^{\circ}}
consisting of distributions extendable to
M
, and its space of section on an open set
V\subset M
is the topological dual of the space of
C^{\infty}
functions on
V
with compact support and with rapid decay along
{\partial M}
, endowed with its usual family of semi-norms.

Near a point
x_{o}\in g^{-1}(0)
where
g^{-1}(0)
has exactly
\ell
components, the space of rapid decay functions decomposes as the direct sum of the spaces of rapid decay functions in each local connected component of
U^{\circ}
, and by duality so does the space of moderate distributions in this neighbourhood. If
x_{o}\in{\partial M}
, then the space of moderate distribution on the intersection of this neighbourhood with
M
is one of the summands.

Definition B.9

(Currents with moderate growth)
.

We set

\mathfrak{C}_{U,p}^{\mathrm{mod}}=\mathscr{E}_{U}^{m-p}\otimes_{\mathscr{C}_{U}^{\infty}}\mathfrak{Db}_{U}^{\mathrm{mod}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{C}_{M,p}^{\mathrm{mod}}=\mathscr{E}_{M}^{m-p}\otimes_{\mathscr{C}_{M}^{\infty}}\mathfrak{Db}_{M}^{\mathrm{mod}}.

In a way similar to currents, a current
T
of dimension
q
with moderate growth on
M
can be paired with a test
q
-form
\varphi
on
M
