with rapid decay and Stokes formula holds.

Proposition B.10

(Poincaré lemma for
(\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\mathrm{mod}},\partial)
)
.

The chain complex
(\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\mathrm{mod}},\partial)
satisfies Poincaré lemma as in Proposition
B.4
.

Proof.

We will first work on
U
and then restrict to
M
, with the cohomological version
(\mathfrak{C}_{M}^{\mathrm{mod},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},{\mathrm{d}})
, that we wish to prove to be a resolution of its
\mathscr{H}^{0}
, that is,
\mathbf{C}_{M}
. In other words, we wish to prove that the natural morphism
(\mathfrak{C}_{M}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\mathrm{d}})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(\mathfrak{C}_{M}^{\mathrm{mod},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},{\mathrm{d}})
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Lemma B.11
.

The complex
(\mathfrak{C}_{[g^{-1}(0)]}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\mathrm{d}})
has cohomology in degree one only and, for
x_{o}
in the intersection of
\ell
smooth components of
g^{-1}(0)
, we have
\dim\mathscr{H}^{1}(\mathfrak{C}_{[g^{-1}(0)]}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\mathrm{d}})_{x_{o}}=2^{\ell}-1
.

Proof.

Let
Z\subset U
denote the
\ell
-th intersection
defined by
x_{1}=\cdots=x_{\ell}=0
,
so that
\mathfrak{Db}_{[Z]}\simeq\mathfrak{Db}_{Z}[\partial_{x_{1}},\dots,\partial_{x_{\ell}}]
. We write
(\mathfrak{C}_{[Z]}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\mathrm{d}})
as the simple complex associated to the
\ell
-cube with
i
-th edges all equal to

\mathfrak{C}_{Z}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}[\partial_{x_{1}},\dots,\partial_{x_{\ell}}]\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\kern 4.0pt\partial_{x_{i}}\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{x_{i}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}\mathfrak{C}_{Z}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}[\partial_{x_{1}},\dots,\partial_{x_{\ell}}],

which is quasi-isomorphic to
\mathfrak{C}_{Z}^{{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}-\ell}
, so
\mathfrak{C}_{[Z]}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}
has cohomology in degree
\ell
only, equal to the constant sheaf
\mathbf{C}_{Z}
(by the Poincaré lemma for
Z
).

Let us set
Z_{i}=\{x_{i}=0\}
and
Z^{(\ell)}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}Z_{i}
. We prove by induction on
\ell
that the lemma hods for
Z^{(\ell)}
, the case
\ell=1
begin proved in the first point. According to
[
39
, Prop. VII.1.4]
, the natural sequence of complexes

0\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(\mathfrak{C}_{[Z_{\ell}^{(\ell-1)}]}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\mathrm{d}})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(\mathfrak{C}_{[Z_{\ell}]}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\mathrm{d}})\oplus(\mathfrak{C}_{[Z^{(\ell-1)}]}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\mathrm{d}})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(\mathfrak{C}_{[Z^{(\ell)}]}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},{\mathrm{d}})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0

is exact. Let us restrict the complexes on
Z=\bigcap_{i=1}^{\ell}Z_{i}
. By induction, the middle complex has cohomology in degree one only, of dimension
1+(2^{\ell-1}-1)=2^{\ell-1}
. Also by induction, the left complex has cohomology in degree
2
only, of dimension
2^{\ell-1}-1
. This completes the proof.
∎

The exact sequence (
B.8
) gives rise to an exact sequence of complexes

0\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathfrak{C}_{[g^{-1}(0)]}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathfrak{C}_{U}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathfrak{C}_{U}^{\mathrm{mod},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0

and the lemma above reduces it to an exact sequence

0\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{C}_{U}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{H}^{0}(\mathfrak{C}_{U}^{\mathrm{mod},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{H}^{1}(\mathfrak{C}_{[g^{-1}(0)]}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\penalty 0.

At a point
x_{o}\in g^{-1}(0)
at the intersection of exactly
\ell
smooth components,
\mathscr{H}^{0}(\mathfrak{C}_{U}^{\mathrm{mod},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}})_{x_{o}}
has thus dimension
2^{\ell}
. If
U^{\circ}_{x_{o},a}
are the
2^{\ell}
local connected components of
U^{\circ}_{x_{o}}
, then

\mathscr{H}^{0}(\mathfrak{C}_{U}^{\mathrm{mod},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}})_{x_{o}}=\mathrm{H}^{0}(\mathfrak{C}^{\mathrm{mod},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}(U^{\circ}_{x_{o}}))=\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{a}\mathrm{H}^{0}(\mathfrak{C}^{\mathrm{mod},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}(U^{\circ}_{x_{o},a})),

so that each term in the sum is isomorphic to
\mathbf{C}
. In particular, for
x_{o}\in{\partial M}
, considering the component
M^{\circ}_{x_{o}}
, the natural composed morphism (after sheaf-theoretically restricting to
M
)
\mathfrak{C}_{U|M}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathfrak{C}_{U|M}^{\mathrm{mod},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathfrak{C}_{M}^{\mathrm{mod},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}
induces an isomorphism on the
\mathscr{H}^{0}
at each
x_{o}
, and hence is an isomorphism.
∎

We define the complex of currents with rapid decay as
(\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\mathrm{mod}},\partial)\otimes(\mathscr{E}_{M}^{\mathrm{rd},{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},{\mathrm{d}})
, with boundary operator given by (
B.4
). From Propositions
B.10
and
B.2
, we thus obtain:

Proposition B.12

(Poincaré lemma for
(\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\mathrm{rd}},\partial)
)
.

The chain complex
(\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\mathrm{rd}},\partial)
has homology in degree
m
only, which is equal to
j_{!}\mathbf{C}_{M^{\circ}}
.

Remark B.13
.

If
M
is compact, the homology of the complex
(\Gamma(M,\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\mathrm{rd}}),\partial)
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{H}_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(M^{\circ},\mathbf{C})
, while that of
(\Gamma(M,\mathfrak{C}_{M,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}^{\mathrm{mod}}),\partial)
is isomorphic to the Borel-Moore homology
\mathrm{H}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BM}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(M^{\circ},\mathbf{C})
.

B.d.
Dolbeault lemmas

We recall here the various Dolbeault lemmas that we have used in the main text. Let
X
be a complex manifold, let
D
be a normal crossing divisor and let
\varpi:\widetilde{X}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}X
be the real oriented blow-up of the components of
D
(local coordinates on
\widetilde{X}
are local polar coordinates on
X
with respect to a coordinate system adapted to
D
). Then
\widetilde{X}
is a
C^{\infty}
manifold with corners as in the beginning of this section, so is endowed with the sheaves
\mathscr{C}^{\infty,\mathrm{mod}}_{\widetilde{X}}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\infty,\mathrm{rd}}_{\widetilde{X}}
as in Definition
B.1
. Moreover, one checks that the
\overline{\partial}
operator defined on
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{X}
can be lifted to these sheaves of functions, and we can define the corresponding Dolbeault complexes
(\mathscr{E}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\mathrm{mod},0,{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},\overline{\partial})
and
(\mathscr{E}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\mathrm{rd},0,{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},\overline{\partial})
whose
\mathscr{H}^{0}
are respectively denoted by
\mathscr{A}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\mathrm{mod}}
and
\mathscr{A}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\mathrm{rd}}
.

Lemma B.14
.

These Dolbeault complexes are a resolution of their
\mathscr{H}^{0}
.

Proof.

For the moderate case, one can use the proof of Dolbeault’s lemma on bounded domains of
\mathbf{C}^{n}
with piecewise
C^{1}
boundary using the Bochner-Martinelli kernel (see e.g.
[
34
, §III.2]
). The rapid decay case is treated in
[
37
]
(see also
[
50
, Lem. II.1.1.18]
and
[
45
, Prop. 4.2.2]
).
∎

One can similarly define the sheaves on
X
of
C^{\infty}
functions with moderate growth or rapid decay along
D
, together with their corresponding Dolbeault complexes, which do not enter the frame considered at the beginning of this section. Nevertheless, one has obviously

(\mathscr{E}_{X}^{\mathrm{mod},0,{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},\overline{\partial})\simeq(\varpi_{*}\mathscr{E}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\mathrm{mod},0,{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},\overline{\partial})\quad\text{and}\quad(\mathscr{E}_{X}^{\mathrm{rd},0,{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},\overline{\partial})\simeq(\varpi_{*}\mathscr{E}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\mathrm{rd},0,{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},\overline{\partial}).

Furthermore,
\varpi_{*}\mathscr{A}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\mathrm{mod}}\simeq\mathscr{O}_{X}(*D)
.

Lemma B.15
.

The Dolbeault complex
(\mathscr{E}_{X}^{\mathrm{mod},0,{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},\overline{\partial})
is a resolution of
\mathscr{O}_{X}(*D)
, and the Dolbeault complex
(\mathscr{E}_{X}^{\mathrm{rd},0,{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}},\overline{\partial})
is quasi-isomorphic to the two-term complex
\mathscr{O}_{X}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{O}_{\widehat{D}}
.

Proof.

The moderate case is treated as in that on
\widetilde{X}
, while the rapid decay case is proved more generally in
[
4
]
.
∎

Appendix C
Remarks on Verdier duality

To pass from local results on pairings to global ones, we use compatibility of Verdier duality with proper pushforward. In this section is made precise an “obvious result” (Corollary
C.6
) which we could not find in the literature. We refer to
[
28
, Chap. 2 & 3]
for standard results of sheaf theory.

We fix a field
{\boldsymbol{k}}
and we work in the category of sheaves of
{\boldsymbol{k}}
-vector spaces. All topological spaces we consider are assumed to be locally compact, and all maps are assumed to have finite local cohomological dimension. Let
\mathbf{D}_{X}
be the dualizing complex. If
a_{X}:X\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{pt}
denotes the constant map, one has
\mathbf{D}_{X}=a_{X}^{!}{\boldsymbol{k}}
.

C.a.
The duality isomorphism

Let
f:X\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}Y
be a continuous map, let
\mathscr{F}
be an object of
\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}({\boldsymbol{k}}_{X})
and
\mathscr{G}
an object of
\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}({\boldsymbol{k}}_{Y})
. There is a bifunctorial isomorphism

(C.1)

\boldsymbol{R}f_{*}\boldsymbol{R}\!\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},f^{!}\mathscr{G})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{R}\!\mathscr{H}\!om(\boldsymbol{R}f_{!}\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})

in
\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}({\boldsymbol{k}}_{Y})
(see
[
28
, Prop. 3.1.10]
). By applying
\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma(Y,{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})
, one obtains

\boldsymbol{R}\Hom(\mathscr{F},f^{!}\mathscr{G})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{R}\Hom(\boldsymbol{R}f_{!}\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}),

and taking cohomology in degree
0
,

\Hom_{\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)}(\mathscr{F},f^{!}\mathscr{G})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Hom_{\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)}(\boldsymbol{R}f_{!}\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}).

There exists a natural morphism of functors
\boldsymbol{R}f_{!}f^{!}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\id
. Indeed, taking
\mathscr{F}=f^{!}\mathscr{G}
above, one finds

\Hom_{\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)}(f^{!}\mathscr{G},f^{!}\mathscr{G})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Hom_{\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)}(\boldsymbol{R}f_{!}f^{!}\mathscr{G},\mathscr{G})

and the desired morphism is the image of the identity.

One can thus write the duality isomorphism (
C.1
) as the composition of two bifunctorial morphisms (see
[
28
, (2.6.25)]
for the first one)

\boldsymbol{R}f_{*}\boldsymbol{R}\!\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},f^{!}\mathscr{G})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{R}\!\mathscr{H}\!om(\boldsymbol{R}f_{!}\mathscr{F},\boldsymbol{R}f_{!}f^{!}\mathscr{G})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{R}\!\mathscr{H}\!om(\boldsymbol{R}f_{!}\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}).

C.b.
Poincaré-Verdier duality

We set
\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{F}=\boldsymbol{R}\!\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathbf{D}_{X})
. One then has an isomorphism

\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma(X,\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{F})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Hom(\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F}),{\boldsymbol{k}}),

and taking hypercohomology,

\boldsymbol{H}^{\ell}(X,\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{F})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{H}^{-\ell}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F})^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}.

Via this isomorphism and by using the natural duality pairing, we deduce a perfect pairing

(C.2)

\boldsymbol{H}^{-\ell}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F})\otimes\boldsymbol{H}^{\ell}(X,\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\boldsymbol{k}}.

On the other hand, one has a natural morphism
\mathscr{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{F}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{D}_{X}

and, in addition,
according to
[
28
, (2.6.23)]
for
f=a_{X}
, a morphism

\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F})\otimes\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma(X,\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{F}).

By composing, one obtains a morphism

\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F})\otimes\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma(X,\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{D}_{X})=\boldsymbol{R}a_{X!}a_{X}^{!}{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\boldsymbol{k}}.

By taking hypercohomology, one obtains for each
\ell\in\mathbf{Z}
a pairing

(C.3)

\boldsymbol{H}^{-\ell}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F})\otimes\boldsymbol{H}^{\ell}(X,\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\boldsymbol{k}}.

Proposition C.4
.

The pairings (
C.2
) and (
C.3
) coincide. In particular, (
C.3
) is a perfect pairing.

For the sake of completeness, we will prove the following lemma at the end of this section.

Lemma C.5
.

Let
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{G}
be objects of
\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}({\boldsymbol{k}}_{X})
and let
f:X\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}Y
be a morphism. The two following composed natural morphisms coincide:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}f_{!}\mathscr{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{R}f_{*}\boldsymbol{R}\!\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{R}f_{!}\bigl(\mathscr{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{R}\!\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})\bigr)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{R}f_{!}\mathscr{G},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}f_{!}\mathscr{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{R}f_{*}\boldsymbol{R}\!\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{R}f_{!}\mathscr{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{R}\!\mathscr{H}\!om(\boldsymbol{R}f_{!}\mathscr{F},\boldsymbol{R}f_{!}\mathscr{G})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{R}f_{!}\mathscr{G}.

Proof of Proposition
C.4
.

Taking
f=a_{X}
and
\mathscr{G}=\mathbf{D}_{X}
, we have equality of the morphisms

\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F})\otimes\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma(X,\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{D}_{X})

and

\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F})\otimes\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma(X,\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{F})\\
\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F})\otimes\Hom(\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F}),\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{D}_{X}))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{D}_{X}).

Besides, we have a commutative diagram, by considering the natural morphism
\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{D}_{X})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\boldsymbol{k}}
,

\textstyle{\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F})\otimes\Hom(\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F}),\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{D}_{X}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{D}_{X}).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F})\otimes\Hom(\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F}),{\boldsymbol{k}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\boldsymbol{k}}

Hence, both composed natural morphisms

\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F})\otimes\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma(X,\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\boldsymbol{k}}

and

\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F})\otimes\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma(X,\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F})\otimes\Hom(\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F}),{\boldsymbol{k}})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\boldsymbol{k}}

coincide, and we deduce the proposition for every
\ell
.
∎

Let now
\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}
in
\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}({\boldsymbol{k}}_{X})
. We have a natural isomorphism (see
[
28
, (2.6.8)]
)

\Hom(\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{G},\mathbf{D}_{X})\simeq\Hom(\mathscr{F},\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{G})\simeq\Hom(\mathscr{G},\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{F}).

Giving a pairing
\varphi:\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{G}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{D}_{X}
amounts thus to giving a morphism
\mathscr{F}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{G}
, or as well a morphism
\mathscr{G}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{F}
. We say that the pairing
\varphi
is
perfect
if the corresponding morphism
\mathscr{F}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{G}
(or as well
\mathscr{G}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{F}
) is an isomorphism.

A pairing
\varphi
induces in a natural way a pairing

\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{G})\otimes\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma(X,\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{G})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{D}_{X})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\boldsymbol{k}}

(and a similar pairing by permuting the roles of
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{G}
), and thus, for every
\ell\in\mathbf{Z}
, pairings

\varphi^{\ell}:\boldsymbol{H}^{-\ell}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{G})\otimes\boldsymbol{H}^{\ell}(X,\mathscr{F})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{H}^{0}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{G})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{H}^{0}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{D}_{X})={\boldsymbol{k}}.

Corollary C.6
.

If
\varphi
is a perfect pairing, the pairings
\varphi^{\ell}
are also perfect.

Proof.

By construction, the composition of
\varphi^{\ell}
and the isomorphism

\boldsymbol{H}^{\ell}(X,\mathscr{F})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{H}^{\ell}(X,\boldsymbol{D}\mathscr{G})

induced by
\varphi
is the pairing (
C.3
) for
\mathscr{G}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
C.5
.

We first show that, for sheaves
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{G}
of
{\boldsymbol{k}}
-vector spaces, the natural morphisms

(C.7)

\displaystyle f_{*}\mathscr{F}\otimes f_{*}\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}f_{*}\bigl(\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})\bigr)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}f_{*}\mathscr{G},

(C.8)

\displaystyle f_{*}\mathscr{F}\otimes f_{*}\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}f_{*}\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{H}\!om(f_{*}\mathscr{F},f_{*}\mathscr{G})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}f_{*}\mathscr{G}

coincide. Let us first recall the construction of the natural morphism

\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{G}.

We denote by
[\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})]^{\sim}
the presheaf

U\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\Gamma(U,\mathscr{F})\otimes\Gamma(U,\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}))

whose associated sheaf is
\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})
by definition. Recall that

\Gamma(U,\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}))=\Hom(\mathscr{F}_{|U},\mathscr{G}_{|U})

(compatible families of morphisms
\Hom(\Gamma(U^{\prime},\mathscr{F}),\Gamma(U^{\prime},\mathscr{G}))
for
U^{\prime}
open in
U
), so that there is a forgetful morphism

\Gamma(U,\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Hom(\Gamma(U,\mathscr{F}),\Gamma(U,\mathscr{G})).

By composition, we obtain a morphism

[\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})]^{\sim}(U)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Gamma(U,\mathscr{F})\otimes\Hom(\Gamma(U,\mathscr{F}),\Gamma(U,\mathscr{G}))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Gamma(U,\mathscr{G}).

Since
\mathscr{G}
is a sheaf, it induces a sheaf morphism
\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{G}
. Considering only open sets of the form
f^{-1}(V)
with
V
open in
Y
gives rise to the natural morphism
f_{*}(\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}f_{*}\mathscr{G}
. But the presheaf

V\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}[\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})]^{\sim}(f^{-1}(V))

is identified with the presheaf giving rise to
f_{*}\mathscr{F}\otimes f_{*}\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})
, so

f_{*}(\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}))=f_{*}\mathscr{F}\otimes f_{*}\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}).

We thus have obtained (
C.7
). Finally, the morphism above reads

[f_{*}\mathscr{F}\otimes f_{*}\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})]^{\sim}(V)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Gamma(V,f_{*}\mathscr{G}),

and its sheafification is nothing but (
C.8
).

By suitably restricting to
f
-proper supports, we obtain the non-derived version of the identification of morphisms in the lemma. In order to get the general case, one replaces
\mathscr{G}
with an injective resolution
\mathscr{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}\in\mathsf{D}^{+}({\boldsymbol{k}}_{X})
. It follows that, for
\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}\in\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{b}}({\boldsymbol{k}}_{X})
,
\boldsymbol{R}\!\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})=\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})
is flabby (see
[
28
, Prop. 2.4.6(vii)]
) and, by considering a flabby resolution
\mathscr{F}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\in\mathsf{D}^{+}({\boldsymbol{k}}_{X})
of
\mathscr{F}
, we replace e.g.
\mathscr{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{R}\!\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})
with
\mathscr{F}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\otimes\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})
, so that we can apply the result for sheaves (we use flabbiness of
\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})
since we cannot consider
\mathscr{H}\!om(\mathscr{F}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},\mathscr{G}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})
, as
\boldsymbol{R}\!\mathscr{H}\!om
is not defined on
\mathsf{D}^{+}({\boldsymbol{k}}_{X})\times\mathsf{D}^{+}({\boldsymbol{k}}_{X})
).
∎

Example C.9
.

In the case of a differentiable manifold
M
with corners as in Section
2.a
, the dualizing complex
\mathbf{D}_{M}
can be represented by
j_{!}\mathbf{C}_{M^{\circ}}[m]
. Proposition
B.2
implies that the shifted complex of rapid decay forms
(\mathscr{E}_{M}^{\mathrm{rd},{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\partial)[m]
is a representative of the dualizing complex. Perfectness of
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}\otimes\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}j_{!}\mathbf{C}_{M^{\circ}}
as stated in Assumption
2.1
means perfectness, in the above sense, of the shifted morphism
\varphi:\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{rd}}[m]\otimes\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{mod}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}j_{!}\mathbf{C}_{M^{\circ}}[m]\simeq\mathbf{D}_{M}
.
